AIC, AIC differences and Akaike weights for candidate survival curves

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000751
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of AIC, AIC differences and Akaike weights for candidate survival curves
has calculator text AIC = 2 * k - 2 * lnL; delta = AIC - min(AIC); w = exp(-delta / 2) / sum(exp(-delta / 2))
has common error note Comparing weights from candidate sets of different size. Removing the exponential from the six-curve example leaves the log-normal weight at about 0.44 only because the exponential contributed almost nothing; removing the generalised gamma instead would raise it to about 0.59. Weights from different candidate sets cannot be compared, and the set should be reported with them
has description in the module Takes the maximised log-likelihoods and parameter counts of a set of candidate survival models fitted to the same data, and returns three vectors: AIC, the AIC difference from the best-scoring model and the Akaike weight of each model. It chains HE-FM-AIC-001, HE-FM-AIC-002 and HE-FM-AIC-003, and the second and third steps use the whole vector produced by the step before, so the inputs are lists of models rather than the single values the formulae take.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-AIC-001, r implementation of HE-CF-AIC-001, python implementation of HE-CF-AIC-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-AIC-001
has source in the module Burnham KP, Anderson DR. Multimodel inference: understanding AIC and BIC in model selection. Sociological Methods & Research. 2004;33(2):261-304. Pages 271 to 272, which define the AIC differences, the likelihood of each model given the data and the Akaike weights, normalised to sum to 1 over the model set. https://doi.org/10.1177/0049124104268644
has test Extra parameter that buys exactly one log-likelihood unit (HE-CF-AIC-001)
Six candidate curves for one trial arm of 300 patients (HE-CF-AIC-001)
Weights sum to one (HE-CF-AIC-001)
Ratio of two weights does not depend on the other models (HE-CF-AIC-001)
has title in the module Computational function: AIC, AIC differences and Akaike weights for candidate survival curves
has title key computational-function-aic-aic-differences-and-akaike-weights-for-candidate-survival-curves
identifier HE-CF-AIC-001
isDefinedBy hemo-formulae.owlexternal
parses in HEMO grammar false
uses formula Akaike information criterion from the maximised log-likelihood, AIC difference from the best-scoring candidate model, Akaike weight of a candidate model

Alpha spent and stopping hazard ratio at a group sequential look

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000752
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Alpha spent and stopping hazard ratio at a group sequential look
has calculator text t_k = d_k / D; t_prev = d_prev / D; alpha_OF_k = 2 - 2 * Phi(Phi^-1(1 - alpha / 2) / sqrt(t_k)); alpha_P_k = alpha * log(1 + (exp(1) - 1) * t_k); inc_OF_k = alpha_OF_k - (2 - 2 * Phi(Phi^-1(1 - alpha / 2) / sqrt(t_prev))) for t_prev > 0; inc_OF_k = alpha_OF_k for t_prev = 0; inc_P_k = alpha_P_k - alpha * log(1 + (exp(1) - 1) * t_prev); HR_k = exp(-2 * c_k / sqrt(d_k))
has common error note Taking later boundaries as the normal quantile of the increment. At the second Pocock type look the increment is 0.006569, and its normal quantile is about 2.480, but the correct boundary is 2.368. The shortcut ignores the first look and the correlation between looks, so the boundary is too strict and part of the budget is never used. Boundaries after the first look have to come from software that integrates the joint distribution
has description in the module Takes the counts a trial report gives, deaths at the current and previous looks and the planned final deaths, with the overall one-sided level and the critical value at the look, and returns the cumulative alpha spent under both Lan-DeMets functions, the increment at the look and the hazard ratio needed to stop. It chains HE-FM-ASP-001, HE-FM-ASP-002, HE-FM-ASP-003, HE-FM-ASP-004 and HE-FM-ASP-005. The critical value is an input because, from the second look onwards, it comes from recursive numerical integration over the joint normal distribution of the test statistics, which a spreadsheet formula cannot do; the function computes only the closed-form parts.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-ASP-001, r implementation of HE-CF-ASP-001, python implementation of HE-CF-ASP-001
has mathematical variable d_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, d_prev in HE-CF-ASP-001, D in HE-CF-ASP-001, alpha in HE-CF-ASP-001, c_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, alpha_OF_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, alpha_P_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, inc_OF_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, inc_P_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, HR_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, t_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, t_prev in HE-CF-ASP-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-ASP-001
has source in the module Anderson KM. gsDesign Technical Manual, chapter 8.1 (spending function definitions). Gives the Lan-DeMets O'Brien-Fleming type and Pocock type functions and prints the Pocock type spend and bounds for four equally spaced looks at alpha of 0.025. https://keaven.github.io/gsd-tech-manual/spendfn.html
has test Both functions spend the full level at the final analysis (HE-CF-ASP-001)
First look of the Pocock type plan (HE-CF-ASP-001)
Final analysis of the O'Brien-Fleming type plan (HE-CF-ASP-001)
First-look Pocock type boundary matches the published value (HE-CF-ASP-001)
Second look of the O'Brien-Fleming type plan (HE-CF-ASP-001)
has title in the module Computational function: alpha spent and stopping hazard ratio at a group sequential look
has title key computational-function-alpha-spent-and-stopping-hazard-ratio-at-a-group-sequential-look
identifier HE-CF-ASP-001
isDefinedBy hemo-formulae.owlexternal
parses in HEMO grammar true
requires input d_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, d_prev in HE-CF-ASP-001, D in HE-CF-ASP-001, alpha in HE-CF-ASP-001, c_k in HE-CF-ASP-001
returns output alpha_OF_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, alpha_P_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, inc_OF_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, inc_P_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, HR_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, t_k in HE-CF-ASP-001, t_prev in HE-CF-ASP-001
uses formula Information fraction from deaths for alpha spending, Lan-DeMets O'Brien-Fleming type spending function, Lan-DeMets Pocock type spending function, Alpha spending increment at an interim look, Hazard ratio needed to cross an alpha spending boundary

Annual event rate to a model-cycle transition probability

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000755
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Annual event rate to a model-cycle transition probability
has calculator text p_cycle = 1 - exp(-r_annual * L_months / 12)
has common error note Dividing the annual rate by 12 and using the result as a probability. Treating the annual rate divided by 12 as the monthly probability overstates it: 0.02 against 0.0198 at a rate of 0.24, and the gap widens as rates rise. The rate has to pass through the exponential conversion
has description in the module Takes the inputs a model usually holds, an event rate per year and a cycle length in months, and returns the transition probability for one cycle. It converts the cycle length into years and then applies the constant-rate formula HE-FM-TP-001. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the formula needs an interval in the same time unit as the rate, and the function builds that interval from the cycle length.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-TP-001, r implementation of HE-CF-TP-001, python implementation of HE-CF-TP-001
has mathematical variable L_months in HE-CF-TP-001, p_cycle in HE-CF-TP-001, r_annual in HE-CF-TP-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-TP-001
has source in the module Gidwani R, Russell LB. Estimating transition probabilities from published evidence: a tutorial for decision modelers. PharmacoEconomics. 2020;38(11):1153-1164. Equations 8 to 10 on converting between rates and probabilities for a chosen time interval. https://doi.org/10.1007/s40273-020-00937-z
has test Monthly cycle from an annual rate of 0.24 (HE-CF-TP-001)
Longer cycles give larger cycle probabilities (HE-CF-TP-001)
Twelve-month cycle reproduces the annual conversion (HE-CF-TP-001)
Zero annual rate gives a zero cycle probability (HE-CF-TP-001)
Twelve monthly cycles compound back to the annual conversion (HE-CF-TP-001)
has title in the module Computational function: annual event rate to a model-cycle transition probability
has title key computational-function-annual-event-rate-to-a-model-cycle-transition-probability
identifier HE-CF-TP-001
isDefinedBy hemo-formulae.owlexternal
parses in HEMO grammar true
requires input L_months in HE-CF-TP-001, r_annual in HE-CF-TP-001
returns output p_cycle in HE-CF-TP-001
uses formula Transition probability from a constant rate

Annual probability to a model-cycle transition probability

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000756
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Annual probability to a model-cycle transition probability
has calculator text r_annual = -log(1 - p_annual); p_cycle = 1 - exp(-r_annual * L_months / 12)
has common error note Passing an annual probability into the rate function. Entering an annual probability of 0.20 as if it were a rate gives a monthly probability of about 0.0165 instead of 0.0184, because the probability understates the underlying rate. The input has to go through the probability to rate step first
has description in the module Takes an annual probability, the form in which many sources report risk, and a cycle length in months, and returns the cycle probability in two steps. It first recovers the implied constant rate with HE-FM-TP-002 and then converts that rate to the cycle probability with HE-FM-TP-001, so two formulae are chained inside one function and the second step uses the result of the first. The answer equals the single-step rescaling formula HE-FM-TP-003, which gives a cross-check.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-TP-002, r implementation of HE-CF-TP-002, python implementation of HE-CF-TP-002
has mathematical variable p_annual in HE-CF-TP-002, L_months in HE-CF-TP-002, p_cycle in HE-CF-TP-002, r_annual in HE-CF-TP-002
has module record identifier HE-CF-TP-002
has source in the module Chhatwal J, Jayasuriya S, Elbasha EH. Changing cycle lengths in state-transition models: challenges and solutions. Medical Decision Making. 2016;36(8):952-964. Section on the traditional approach, which converts the probability to a rate and back for the new cycle length and gives the equivalent rescaling formula. https://doi.org/10.1177/0272989X16656165
has test Annual risk of 0.20 in a monthly model (HE-CF-TP-002)
Twelve-month cycle returns the annual probability (HE-CF-TP-002)
Two-step result matches the single-step rescaling formula (HE-CF-TP-002)
has title in the module Computational function: annual probability to a model-cycle transition probability
has title key computational-function-annual-probability-to-a-model-cycle-transition-probability
identifier HE-CF-TP-002
isDefinedBy hemo-formulae.owlexternal
parses in HEMO grammar true
requires input p_annual in HE-CF-TP-002, L_months in HE-CF-TP-002
returns output p_cycle in HE-CF-TP-002, r_annual in HE-CF-TP-002
uses formula Transition probability from a constant rate, Constant rate from a transition probability, Transition probability rescaled to a new cycle length, Destination probabilities under constant competing hazards

Antithetic pairs of exponential survival times with a pair-mean standard error

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000763
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Antithetic pairs of exponential survival times with a pair-mean standard error
has calculator text T = -log(U) / h; T_anti = -log(1 - U) / h; Tbar = (T + T_anti) / 2; mu_anti = mean(Tbar); SE_anti = sd(Tbar) / sqrt(m)
has common error note Reflecting the survival time instead of the uniform number. Mirroring a sampled survival time about its mean of 10 years, instead of applying 1-U before the transformation, gives a partner of -3.026 years for a draw of 23.026 years, which is impossible. The complement has to be taken on the uniform scale
has description in the module Takes a hazard and a vector of uniform random numbers, one per pair, and returns the antithetic estimate of mean survival and its standard error. For each uniform number U it samples a survival time by the inverse distribution function and a partner from 1-U, averages the two, and treats the pair means as an independent sample, so it combines HE-FM-ANTV-001 with the pair-mean standard error of HE-FM-ANTV-002. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: sigma2 and rho are not entered but follow from the exponential distribution, with rho equal to 1-pi^2/6.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-ANTV-001, r implementation of HE-CF-ANTV-001, python implementation of HE-CF-ANTV-001
has mathematical variable h in HE-CF-ANTV-001, U in HE-CF-ANTV-001, T in HE-CF-ANTV-001, T_anti in HE-CF-ANTV-001, Tbar in HE-CF-ANTV-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-ANTV-001
has source in the module Owen AB. Monte Carlo Theory, Methods and Examples. 2013. Chapter 8, section 8.2, which defines the antithetic counterpart of a uniform point as one minus each coordinate and estimates the variance of the antithetic estimate from the pair means. https://artowen.su.domains/mc/Ch-var-basic.pdf
has test Pair drawn at U = 0.20 (HE-CF-ANTV-001)
Pair drawn at U = 0.90 (HE-CF-ANTV-001)
Midpoint draw gives identical antithetic partners (HE-CF-ANTV-001)
Pair-mean standard error below the naive value (HE-CF-ANTV-001)
Two pairs combined (HE-CF-ANTV-001)
Sample correlation close to its theoretical value (HE-CF-ANTV-001)
has title in the module Computational function: antithetic pairs of exponential survival times with a pair-mean standard error
has title key computational-function-antithetic-pairs-of-exponential-survival-times-with-a-pair-mean-standard-error
identifier HE-CF-ANTV-001
isDefinedBy hemo-formulae.owlexternal
parses in HEMO grammar false
requires input h in HE-CF-ANTV-001, U in HE-CF-ANTV-001
returns output T in HE-CF-ANTV-001, T_anti in HE-CF-ANTV-001, Tbar in HE-CF-ANTV-001
uses formula Variance of the antithetic variates estimator with within-pair correlation
Standard error of the antithetic estimate from pair means

AR(1) QALY variance and between-arm standard error for any visit schedule

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000760
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of AR(1) QALY variance and between-arm standard error for any visit schedule
has calculator text w_j = (t_(min(j+1, T)) - t_(max(j-1, 1))) / 2; Var_Q = sigma2 * sum_j sum_k w_j * w_k * phi^abs(t_j - t_k); SE = sqrt(2 * Var_Q / n)
has common error note Counting visit steps when visits are unequally spaced. In an illustrative 0, 3, 6, 12-month schedule, treating every step as a correlation of 0.7 gives the baseline and 12-month utilities a correlation of 0.343 rather than 0.49, and a QALY variance of about 0.02865 rather than 0.03164, about 9 per cent too low. Elapsed time, not visit number, belongs in the exponent
has description in the module Takes a visit schedule in years, the utility variance at each visit, a correlation per year and the number of patients per arm, and returns the variance of each patient's QALY total and the standard error of the difference in mean QALYs between two arms. It derives the trapezium weights from the visit times, builds the continuous-time AR(1) correlation matrix of HE-FM-AR1-002 from the elapsed times, and evaluates the double sum behind HE-FM-AR1-003 for any number of visits. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the weights and the correlations are computed from the schedule rather than entered.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-AR1-001, r implementation of HE-CF-AR1-001, python implementation of HE-CF-AR1-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-AR1-001
has source in the module Gabrio A, Plumpton C, Banerjee S, Leurent B. Linear mixed models to handle missing at random data in trial-based economic evaluations. Health Economics. 2022;31(6):1276-1287. Appendix C, which computes QALYs with weights equal to half the time between adjacent visits. Liang KY, Zeger SL. Biometrika. 1986;73(1):13-22. Section 4, Example 4, the continuous-time AR-1 working correlation for any number and spacing of observations. https://doi.org/10.1002/hec.4510
has test Perfect correlation limiting case (HE-CF-AR1-001)
Three visits with 100 patients per arm (HE-CF-AR1-001)
Unequally spaced visits at 0, 3, 6 and 12 months (HE-CF-AR1-001)
Weights add up to the length of follow-up (HE-CF-AR1-001)
Variance lies between the independence and perfect-correlation values (HE-CF-AR1-001)
has title in the module Computational function: AR(1) QALY variance and between-arm standard error for any visit schedule
has title key computational-function-ar-1-qaly-variance-and-between-arm-standard-error-for-any-visit-schedule
identifier HE-CF-AR1-001
isDefinedBy hemo-formulae.owlexternal
parses in HEMO grammar true
uses formula Continuous-time AR(1) correlation for unequally spaced visits, Variance of a three-visit QALY total under AR(1) correlation

Atkinson index from subgroup health levels and population shares

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000757
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Atkinson index from subgroup health levels and population shares
has calculator text h_bar = sum_(g=1)^G [s_g * h_g]; h_EDE = (sum_(g=1)^G [s_g * h_g^(1-epsilon)])^(1/(1-epsilon)); A_epsilon = 1 - h_EDE / h_bar
has common error note Weighting the mean but not the EDE by population share. Using share-weighted mean health with an EDE that counts each group once mixes two distributions. In the unequal-shares example it gives an index of about 0.0596 instead of 0.00531, overstating inequality more than tenfold
has description in the module Takes the inputs a distributional cost-effectiveness analysis usually holds, the mean health of each population subgroup and the share of the population in each subgroup, together with a chosen epsilon, and returns mean health, the equally distributed equivalent health and the Atkinson index. It weights each group by its population share, as in Atkinson's measure for discrete distributions, and switches to the share-weighted geometric mean when epsilon equals 1, where the general formula HE-FM-ATK-002 is undefined. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: HE-FM-ATK-002 and HE-FM-ATK-003 need one value per person or per equal-sized group, and the function accepts groups of any size.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-ATK-001, r implementation of HE-CF-ATK-001, python implementation of HE-CF-ATK-001
has mathematical variable A_epsilon in HE-CF-ATK-001, h_g in HE-CF-ATK-001, G in HE-CF-ATK-001, epsilon in HE-CF-ATK-001, h_bar in HE-CF-ATK-001, h_EDE in HE-CF-ATK-001, s_g in HE-CF-ATK-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-ATK-001
has source in the module Atkinson AB. On the measurement of inequality. Journal of Economic Theory. 1970;2(3):244-263. Page 257, which gives the measure for discrete distributions with each income level weighted by its frequency in the distribution. https://doi.org/10.1016/0022-0531(70)90039-6
has test Epsilon of 1 gives the geometric mean (HE-CF-ATK-001), Groups of unequal size (HE-CF-ATK-001), Programme U, two equal-sized groups, epsilon of 2 (HE-CF-ATK-001), Splitting a group leaves the result unchanged (HE-CF-ATK-001)
has title in the module Computational function: Atkinson index from subgroup health levels and population shares
has title key computational-function-atkinson-index-from-subgroup-health-levels-and-population-shares

Attributable annual cost of prevalent cases from survey counts and a prevalence ratio

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000834
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Attributable annual cost of prevalent cases from survey counts and a prevalence ratio
has calculator text P_1 = a / (a + b); P_0 = c / (c + d); PR = P_1 / P_0; PAF = e * (PR - 1) / (1 + e * (PR - 1)); M = N * (e * P_1 + (1 - e) * P_0); A = PAF * M; C_A = A * k
has common error note Using the prevalence odds ratio from a logistic model. Feeding the survey POR of about 2.4286 into the attributable fraction raises the attributable cases to about 38,182 and the cost to GBP 45.8 million, 27% above the figure from the PR, because the condition is common
has description in the module Takes the four counts of a cross-sectional survey and three facts about a local population, its size, its exposed share and an annual cost per prevalent case, and returns the attributable annual cost of prevalent cases. It chains three steps: the prevalence ratio HE-FM-PR-001 from the survey, the attributable fraction HE-FM-PR-003 with the local exposed share, and the top-down burden HE-FM-COI-002 applied to the local prevalent cases. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the survey supplies the ratio and the prevalences, and the local population supplies the cases and costs.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-PR-001, r implementation of HE-CF-PR-001, python implementation of HE-CF-PR-001

Section on top-down, bottom-up and econometric approaches (PAF from a prevalence and an unadjusted relative risk, applied to aggregate costs); Tamhane AR, Westfall AO, Burkholder GA, Cutter GR. Prevalence odds ratio versus prevalence ratio: choice comes with consequences. Statistics in Medicine. 2016;35(30):5730-5735. Table 3 (prevalence ratio from a two-by-two table).

AUC from predicted risks and observed outcomes

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000758
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of AUC from predicted risks and observed outcomes
has calculator text n_1 = sum(y); n_0 = sum(1 - y); n_conc = sum_(i: y_i=1) sum_(j: y_j=0) [1(s_i > s_j)]; n_tie = sum_(i: y_i=1) sum_(j: y_j=0) [1(s_i = s_j)]; AUC = (n_conc + 0.5 * n_tie) / (n_1 * n_0)
has common error note Dividing by every pair of patients. Comparing all 45 pairs among the ten patients, rather than only the 24 pairs of one case and one non-case, gives 21.5 divided by 45, about 0.478, which wrongly suggests a score worse than chance
has description in the module Takes the data a validation study holds, one predicted risk or test score and one observed outcome per person, and returns the AUC. It splits the people into those with and without the outcome, compares every case with every non-case, counts the concordant and tied pairs and then applies HE-FM-AUC-001. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the formula needs pair counts, and the function builds them from patient-level data.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-AUC-001, r implementation of HE-CF-AUC-001, python implementation of HE-CF-AUC-001
has mathematical variable AUC in HE-CF-AUC-001, n_1 in HE-CF-AUC-001, n_0 in HE-CF-AUC-001, n_conc in HE-CF-AUC-001, n_tie in HE-CF-AUC-001, s in HE-CF-AUC-001, y in HE-CF-AUC-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-AUC-001
has source in the module Hanley JA, McNeil BJ. The meaning and use of the area under a receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve. Radiology. 1982;143(1):29-36. The area equals the probability of ranking a randomly chosen diseased subject above a randomly chosen non-diseased subject, the quantity estimated by the Wilcoxon statistic. https://doi.org/10.1148/radiology.143.1.7063747
has test Every predicted risk 0.10 higher (HE-CF-AUC-001), Pair count matches the trapezoidal ROC area (HE-CF-AUC-001), Order-preserving shift leaves the AUC unchanged (HE-CF-AUC-001), Ten-patient risk score (HE-CF-AUC-001), Score reversed (HE-CF-AUC-001)

Average length of stay from admission and discharge dates

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000761
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Average length of stay from admission and discharge dates
has calculator text L_i = d_i - a_i; BD = sum(L_i); L_bar = BD / N
has common error note Selecting stays by admission date. Choosing the stays admitted in March in the first example drops the 11-day stay that began in February and adds the 10-day stay that ended in April, giving 14 bed-days over 3 stays and an ALOS of about 4.67 days instead of 5. ALOS is taken over the discharges in the period
has description in the module Takes patient-level admission and discharge dates and a reporting period, and returns the average length of stay for the discharges in that period. It selects stays by discharge date, computes each stay as the number of changes of calendar day from admission to discharge, sums them to bed-days and divides by the number of discharges, so its inputs are dates rather than the bed-day and discharge totals of HE-FM-ALOS-001. A stay that began before the period is counted in full, and a same-day stay counts as zero days.

Begg test from study effect estimates and standard errors

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000794
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Begg test from study effect estimates and standard errors
has calculator text W = sum_(j=1)^K [1 / v_j]; theta_IV = sum_(j=1)^K [theta_j / v_j] / W; t_j = (theta_j - theta_IV) / sqrt(v_j - 1 / W); S = sum_(j<k) [sign((t_j - t_k) * (v_j - v_k))]; tau = S / (K * (K - 1) / 2); Z = S / sqrt(K * (K - 1) * (2 * K + 5) / 18)
has common error note Leaving out the variance correction in the standardisation. Dividing each deviation by the study's own standard error instead of the square root of its variance less one over the sum of weights changes the ranks in the article's example to 12 concordant and 33 discordant pairs, so S is -21 and Z about -1.88, a p-value of about 0.060 instead of 0.040 (computed here for illustration)
has description in the module Takes the effect estimates of K studies on an approximately normal scale and their standard errors, and returns Kendall's tau, the normal statistic and its two-sided p-value. It computes the inverse-variance weights and pooled estimate (HE-FM-ADMA-001), standardises each estimate (HE-FM-BEGG-002), compares every pair of studies once by the signs of their differences in standardised effect and in variance, and applies HE-FM-BEGG-001 to the counts. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the formula needs the pair counts, and the function builds them from the raw estimates.
has formalisation level catalogue record

has mathematical variable effect in HE-CF-BEGG-001, p in HE-CF-BEGG-001, se in HE-CF-BEGG-001, tau in HE-CF-BEGG-001, Z in HE-CF-BEGG-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-BEGG-001
has source in the module StataCorp. Stata Meta-Analysis Reference Manual: meta bias. College Station, TX: StataCorp LLC; 2025. Methods and formulas, Begg's rank correlation test: the standardised effect sizes, the inverse-variance pooled estimate, the variance of the difference between each estimate and the pooled estimate, and the test as Kendall's rank correlation test of independence between the standardised effects and their variances. https://www.stata.com/manuals/metametabias.pdf

The count may be the number of uncensored events, as in HE-FM-BIC-001, or the number of patients, which gives the patient-count form recorded on the Akaike information criterion page.

has common error note One run at averaged transition probabilities. Outcomes are not linear in transition probabilities. If half a cohort survives each cycle with probability 0.9 and half with 0.5, the expected cycles alive counted at the start of each cycle are 10 and 2, a mean of 6, whereas one run at the average of 0.7 gives about 3.33. Subgroups that differ in risk need separate runs, with results weighted afterwards
has description in the module Runs a cohort simulation from the inputs a model holds, a starting distribution, a transition matrix, the reward per cycle in each state, a number of cycles, a counting rule and a discount rate per cycle, and returns the cohort trace and the expected total outcome per person. It first builds the trace row by row with the cohort update HE-FM-MM-001, then forms the counting weights and discount factors and applies HE-FM-CSIM-001. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the formula takes the trace rows, weights and discount factors as given, while the function generates them.

TSD 12 and the article give the rules for two conditions; applying the same rule to more conditions (sum of decrements, product of ratios, lowest value) is the convention stated here; TSD 12 section 3.2.1 notes that very little research has explored the accuracy of the methods for more than two simultaneous conditions.

Computational function for a baseline-risk adjusted odds ratio, events avoided and ICER from a centred meta-regression

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000840
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for a baseline-risk adjusted odds ratio, events avoided and ICER from a centred meta-regression
has calculator text z = log(p_0 / (1 - p_0)); theta_z = d + b * (z - x_bar); OR_z = exp(theta_z); p_1 = p_0 / (1 - p_0) * OR_z / (1 + p_0 / (1 - p_0) * OR_z); N_av = 1000 * (p_0 - p_1); C_net = 1000 * c_tx - N_av * s_ev; Q_gain = N_av * q_ev; ICER = C_net / Q_gain
has common error note Multiplying the baseline risk by the odds ratio. Treating the odds ratio as a risk ratio gives a treated risk of p_0 times OR_z. At a 5% risk the error is small, 26.9 instead of 26.3 events avoided per 1,000, but at a 35% risk it gives 25.5 instead of 17.0, half as many again. The odds ratio has to be applied to the baseline odds
has description in the module Takes the inputs a decision modeller holds, a baseline risk for the model population, the fitted coefficients of a centred meta-regression on baseline log-odds and the cost and QALY consequences of an event, and returns the adjusted odds ratio, the absolute effect and the incremental cost-effectiveness ratio. It converts the risk to log-odds, applies HE-FM-MREG-005, exponentiates the result and applies the odds ratio to the baseline odds, so each step uses the result of the one before. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables, which need the covariate already on the log-odds scale.

Each band's sum is geometric, so the calculator line adds three closed-form sums, and for a horizon of 30 years or less the standard sum is the annuity of HE-FM-DISC-001. has common error note Weight reference shares used without price-updating. The manual's Lowe index takes weights from period b and price-updates them to the base period (section 3.3). Using the raw shares 0.5, 0.3 and 0.2 in the first worked example gives 106.5 in place of 106.4593 (computed here for illustration), a gap that depends on how far relative prices moved between period b and the base period
has description in the module Takes the household expenditure on each item in the weight reference period b, the form in which the national accounts supply weights, with item prices in period b, in the base period 0 and at time t. It price-updates each expenditure to the base period, turns the updated amounts into weights and averages the price relatives with them, the steps of sections 3.3 and 3.5 of the ONS technical manual. The result equals the basket form of HE-FM-CPI-001 with quantities V_b / p_b, so the inputs differ from the formula's variables, which are quantities.

Computational function for a PSA run with correlated log baseline rate and log hazard ratio through a constant-rate survival model

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000872
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for a PSA run with correlated log baseline rate and log hazard ratio through a constant-rate survival model
has calculator text b0_1 = mu_b0 + s_b0 * z1_1; b1_1 = mu_b1 + s_b1 * (rho * z1_1 + sqrt(1 - rho^2) * z2_1); INHB_1 = q * (exp(-b0_1 - b1_1) - exp(-b0_1)) - dC / lambda; b0_2 = mu_b0 + s_b0 * z1_2; b1_2 = mu_b1 + s_b1 * (rho * z1_2 + sqrt(1 - rho^2) * z2_2); INHB_2 = q * (exp(-b0_2 - b1_2) - exp(-b0_2)) - dC / lambda; b0_3 = mu_b0 + s_b0 * z1_3; b1_3 = mu_b1 + s_b1 * (rho * z1_3 + sqrt(1 - rho^2) * z2_3); INHB_3 = q * (exp(-b0_3 - b1_3) - exp(-b0_3)) - dC / lambda; b0_4 = mu_b0 + s_b0 * z1_4; b1_4 = mu_b1 + s_b1 * (rho * z1_4 + sqrt(1 - rho^2) * z2_4); INHB_4 = q * (exp(-b0_4 - b1_4) - exp(-b0_4)) - dC / lambda; b0_5 = mu_b0 + s_b0 * z1_5; b1_5 = mu_b1 + s_b1 * (rho * z1_5 + sqrt(1 - rho^2) * z2_5); INHB_5 = q * (exp(-b0_5 - b1_5) - exp(-b0_5)) - dC / lambda; ENHB = (INHB_1 + INHB_2 + INHB_3 + INHB_4 + INHB_5) / 5; P_CE = ((INHB_1 > 0) + (INHB_2 > 0) + (INHB_3 > 0) + (INHB_4 > 0) + (INHB_5 > 0)) / 5; EL_new = (max(-INHB_1, 0) + max(-INHB_2, 0) + max(-INHB_3, 0) + max(-INHB_4, 0) + max(-INHB_5, 0)) / 5; EL_comp = (max(INHB_1, 0) + max(INHB_2, 0) + max(INHB_3, 0) + max(INHB_4, 0) + max(INHB_5, 0)) / 5; SD_INHB = sqrt(((INHB_1 - ENHB)^2 + (INHB_2 - ENHB)^2 + (INHB_3 - ENHB)^2 + (INHB_4 - ENHB)^2 + (INHB_5 - ENHB)^2) / 4); MCSE = SD_INHB / sqrt(5); N_req = (SD_INHB / e_target)^2
has common error note Fresh baseline draw for the new option. Drawing a separate log baseline rate for the new option while its log hazard ratio is still drawn from the comparator's first standard normal value breaks the correlation of minus 0.5 between the new option's baseline and its hazard ratio, so the log of its treated rate has a variance of 0.05 instead of 0.03 (derived). From the log-normal moments (derived here, and matched by a 4 million draw simulation) the standard deviation of the incremental net health benefit rises from about 0.487 to about 0.693 QALYs, about 2.03 times the variance, so about twice as many draws are needed for the same Monte Carlo standard error (HE-FM-ABM-003), and the expected value moves from about minus 0.0722 to about minus 0.0467 QALYs. The general rule that both options use the same draw is HE-ER-ENB-003
has description in the module Runs a complete probabilistic sensitivity analysis on a small fixed set of draws. For each draw it takes a pair of independent standard normal values, turns them into a correlated log baseline mortality rate and log hazard ratio with the Cholesky step HE-FM-PSA-001, runs a constant-rate survival model for both options with those same parameters, and stores the new option's incremental net health benefit. It then returns the expected incremental net health benefit (HE-FM-ENB-002), the probability that the new option is cost-effective (HE-FM-CEAC-001, with ties counted as not cost-effective), the two expected losses of HE-FM-PSA-002, the Monte Carlo standard error of the mean (the form of HE-FM-ANTV-002 with one observation per draw) and the number of draws for a target standard error (HE-FM-ABM-003). As in the article, mean survival under a constant mortality rate m is 1 / m years and each life-year is worth q QALYs without discounting, so a draw with log rate b0 and log hazard ratio b1 gives an incremental QALY gain of q times exp(minus b0 minus b1) less exp(minus b0). The Math / Code line writes out five draws; the R and Python versions draw any number.

has description in the module Takes the two amounts, a choice of discount form and its parameters, builds the weights at delays 0, 1 and 2, and returns the two margins of HE-FM-HYPD-005, whether the plan reverses, the penalty on the sooner outcome felt now that would tip today's choice, the reward paid with the later outcome that would do the same, and the first whole delay at which waiting is preferred. Computational function for an Euler run of a closed SIR model with and without vaccination counting infections averted statically and dynamically

has calculator text F1b = beta * S_0 * I_0 / N * dt; S1b = S_0 - F1b; I1b = I_0 + F1b - gamma * I_0 * dt; S0p = S_0 - V; F1p = beta * S0p * I_0 / N * dt; S1p = S0p - F1p; I1p = I_0 + F1p - gamma * I_0 * dt; F2b = beta * S1b * I1b / N * dt; F2s = beta * I1b / N * S1p * dt; F2d = beta * S1p * I1p / N * dt; A_s = F1b - F1p + F2b - F2s; A_d = F1b - F1p + F2b - F2d; A_i = A_d - A_s; C_s = c_inf * A_s; C_d = c_inf * A_d
has common error note Horizon cut before the programme run's epidemic ends. A programme can delay infections as well as prevent them. With the article's inputs the dynamic count of infections averted is about 3,493.3 after 30 days but about 2,376.4 once the epidemic has ended in both runs, because infections avoided early in the programme run partly occur later (computed here for illustration). Pitman and colleagues report that preventive control programmes are often very sensitive to the time horizon of the analysis and recommend sensitivity analysis on it
has description in the module Runs a closed SIR model forward in Euler steps of HE-FM-CMPM-004 three times, without a programme, with a programme under a dynamic force of infection and with the same programme under the static force of infection taken from the no-programme run, and sums infections averted by each count over the horizon, as HE-FM-CMPM-005 does for one step. It also returns averted infection costs, optionally discounted with a continuous rate as in HE-FM-CONT-003. has calculator text Se_1 = 1 - x_1D / n_D; Se_2 = 1 - x_2D / n_D; Sp_1 = y_1N / n_N; Sp_2 = y_2N / n_N; C_pos = x_12D / n_D - (1 - Se_1) * (1 - Se_2); C_neg = y_12N / n_N - Sp_1 * Sp_2; LR_neg_12 = ((1 - Se_1) * (1 - Se_2) + C_pos) / (Sp_1 * Sp_2 + C_neg); LR_prod = (1 - Se_1) / Sp_1 * (1 - Se_2) / Sp_2
has common error note Independence product used when covariances can be estimated. has description in the module Takes the counts from a study in which every person had both tests and the reference standard and returns the single-test sensitivities and specificities, the conditional covariances C_pos and C_neg, the joint negative likelihood ratio LR_neg_12 of HE-FM-NLR-002 and the product LR_prod of the single-test ratios for comparison. The inputs are counts, not the formula's accuracy values. With the plug-in covariances the joint ratio equals the observed both-negative proportion among people with the condition divided by that among people without it (derived), so the procedure shows how much of that ratio the independence product misses.

Sonnenberg and Beck note that the probability of death increases as the cohort ages and must change with each cycle
has description in the module Takes a starting age, a cycle length, a number of cycles and a life table that gives an annual probability of death for each age band, and returns the background death probability p_bg for every cycle and the share of the cohort still alive when background causes alone act. For each cycle it finds the band that contains the attained age at the start of the cycle, starting age plus elapsed time, and rescales that band's annual probability to the cycle length with HE-FM-TP-003, so the probabilities rise as the cohort moves into older bands. The vector then feeds HE-FM-BGM-002 cycle by cycle. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables, which describe a single cycle.

Computational function for DALYs averted and cost per DALY averted from deaths, disability and duration in two scenarios

Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for DALYs averted and cost per DALY averted from deaths, disability and duration in two scenarios
has calculator text f_L = (1 - exp(-r * L)) / r; f_T = (1 - exp(-r * T)) / r; DALY_0 = D_0 * f_L + (N - D_0) * DW_0 * f_T; DALY_1 = D_1 * f_L + (N - D_1) * DW_1 * f_T; DALY_av = DALY_0 - DALY_1; share_YLL = (D_0 - D_1) * f_L / DALY_av; CPD = dC / DALY_av

Entering the survivors directly and leaving them at 180 in the programme scenario credits 900 DALYs averted in place of 888 undiscounted and lowers the cost per DALY averted to about USD 493 (computed here for illustration). Setting survivors to the cohort less its deaths avoids this
has description in the module Takes the size of a cohort, its deaths and the survivors' disability weight with and without a programme, the duration of the condition, the standard remaining life expectancy at the age of death, an optional continuous discount rate and the extra cost, and returns each scenario's DALYs, the DALYs averted, the share of them that comes from deaths prevented and the cost per DALY averted. It sets the survivors in each scenario to the cohort less its deaths, so the people saved by the programme are counted as living with the condition, and it chains HE-FM-DALY-002 or HE-FM-DALY-004 with HE-FM-DALY-003. The inputs therefore differ from the formulas' variables, which take survivors and DALY totals as given.

Computational function for delta-adjusted QALY gain, ICER and tipping-point shift over a grid for loss to follow-up

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000878
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for delta-adjusted QALY gain, ICER and tipping-point shift over a grid for loss to follow-up
has calculator text E_T = E_obs_T + p_T * delta; E_C = E_obs_C + r * p_C * delta; Delta_E = E_T - E_C; ICER = Delta_C / Delta_E; INHB = Delta_E - Delta_C / lambda; delta_star = (Delta_C / lambda - (E_obs_T - E_obs_C)) / (p_T - r * p_C)
has common error note Observed means used when the imputation moved the base case. If the participant lost from the new treatment arm in the table above is imputed at 0.70 under missing at random, for example because of a worse baseline, the base-case gain is 0.084 and the tipping point is -0.045. Feeding the observed-mean gain of 0.10 into HE-FM-LTFU-001 returns -0.125 and overstates the shift the conclusion can absorb by a factor of almost three (computed here for illustration)
has description in the module Takes each arm's QALYs after imputation under missing at random, with a flag for the participants lost to follow-up, the incremental cost, the threshold, the shift ratio r and a grid of shifts, adds each shift to the imputed QALYs of those lost (r times the shift in usual care), and returns the delta-adjusted QALY gain, ICER and incremental net health benefit at every shift, with the tipping point found where the net health benefit changes sign by the interpolation of HE-FM-OWSA-002. This is the scan that Leurent and colleagues and Faria and colleagues describe, run on one completed dataset; with several imputed datasets the scan is run on each and the gains are averaged by Rubin's rules. The arm means follow HE-FM-LOCF-001 and HE-FM-LOCF-002 as read in HE-FN-LTFU-001, the ratio is HE-FM-ICER-001 and the net health benefit is HE-FM-CET-002. For an additive shift on QALYs alone the net health benefit is linear in the shift, so the grid returns the closed form of HE-FM-LTFU-001, which the Math / Code line computes from arm summaries. The grid is needed when the conclusion is judged on a quantity that is not linear in the shift, such as the probability of cost-effectiveness from bootstrap samples, or when several parameters are varied together.

Computational function for Erlang B refusals, beds for a refusal target and cost-minimising beds

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000934
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for Erlang B refusals, beds for a refusal target and cost-minimising beds
has calculator text a = lambda * L_bar; B_1 = a / (1 + a); B_2 = a * B_1 / (2 + a * B_1); B_3 = a * B_2 / (3 + a * B_2); B_4 = a * B_3 / (4 + a * B_3); B_5 = a * B_4 / (5 + a * B_4); B_6 = a * B_5 / (6 + a * B_5); R_5 = 365 * lambda * B_5; R_6 = 365 * lambda * B_6; S_6 = c_r * (R_5 - R_6) - c_b
has common error note Factorials in one cell for a large ward. Writing the loss formula with FACT and powers in one cell fails above 170 beds, because FACT returns #NUM! for arguments above 170, and powers of the load overflow for large wards in the same way. The recursion keeps every term at or below 1
One occupancy target for every unit size. Sizing the article's small unit at 85% occupancy gives 10 beds and 12.2% refused, about one patient in eight, while the same rule gives 1.14% for the 95-bed unit; the beds for a stated refusal target come from BedsForTarget, not from the occupancy rule
has description in the module Takes a ward's arrival rate per day, its mean stay, a refusal target and the yearly cost of a staffed bed and of a refused admission, and returns the Erlang loss probability, refusals a year and achieved occupancy for every number of beds up to a ceiling, the smallest number of beds that meets the refusal target and the number of beds that minimises the total cost of beds and refusals. It computes the loss probability of HE-FM-CAPP-001 for any number of beds by the Erlang B recursion B_0 = 1, B_k = a B_(k-1) / (k + a B_(k-1)), which avoids the large powers and factorials of the direct formula, and its target and cost inputs differ from the formula's symbols. The total cost of N beds is c_b N plus c_r times the refusals at N beds, and one more bed is worth adding while the refusals it avoids, times c_r, exceed c_b. Because each extra bed avoids fewer refusals than the one before, the first bed that fails this test marks the cost minimum (derived; it follows from the strict convexity of the loss function in the number of beds that Tuenter reports from Jagers and van Doorn, and a grid check of 75 cases found no exception). The calculator line writes the recursion out for six beds and returns the net saving of the sixth bed; the searches over N are in the Excel, R and Python code.

Computational function for every equilibrium of a 2 x 2 game with the outcome probabilities and expected welfare of mixed play

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000959
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for every equilibrium of a 2 x 2 game with the outcome probabilities and expected welfare of mixed play
has calculator text NE_11 = (u_11 >= u_21) and (v_11 >= v_12); NE_12 = (u_12 >= u_22) and (v_12 >= v_11); NE_21 = (u_21 >= u_11) and (v_21 >= v_22); NE_22 = (u_22 >= u_12) and (v_22 >= v_21); a_1 = u_11 - u_21; a_2 = u_22 - u_12; q_star = a_2 / (a_1 + a_2); b_1 = v_11 - v_12; b_2 = v_22 - v_21; p_star = b_2 / (b_1 + b_2); interior = (q_star > 0) and (q_star < 1) and (p_star > 0) and (p_star < 1); P_11 = p_star * q_star; P_12 = p_star * (1 - q_star); P_21 = (1 - p_star) * q_star; P_22 = (1 - p_star) * (1 - q_star); U_A = q_star * u_11 + (1 - q_star) * u_12; U_B = p_star * v_11 + (1 - p_star) * v_21; E_W = P_11 * W_11 + P_12 * W_12 + P_21 * W_21 + P_22 * W_22
has common error note Equal weights on the four cells. Averaging the cell values as if each outcome were equally likely gives (7 + 14 + 14 + 0) / 4 = 8.75 for the angioplasty game in place of 9.3333 (computed here), and reports a value that no equilibrium produces
has description in the module Lists every equilibrium of a two-player game with two strategies each and values the outcome of mixed play, the procedure Slantchev sets out for finding all Nash equilibria, checking first for pure-strategy solutions and then for mixed ones. The pure cells come from HE-FM-NASH-001 and the completely mixed equilibrium from HE-FM-NASH-002. Under independent mixing each cell's probability is the product of the two players' probabilities, the pathway probability of HE-FM-CHN-002, and the expected payoffs and the expected value of an outcome measure W over the four cells are probability-weighted sums, the form of HE-FM-CHN-001. At the mixed equilibrium each player's expected payoff can be read from its first strategy alone, because both of its strategies pay the same (derived from the indifference condition). The probabilities, payoffs and E_W apply only when interior is 1.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-NASH-001, r implementation of HE-CF-NASH-001, python implementation of HE-CF-NASH-001
has mathematical variable interior in HE-CF-NASH-001, E_W in HE-CF-NASH-001, NE_12 in HE-CF-NASH-001, NE_21 in HE-CF-NASH-001, p_star in HE-CF-NASH-001, P_11 in HE-CF-NASH-001, P_12 in HE-CF-NASH-001, P_21 in HE-CF-NASH-001, P_22 in HE-CF-NASH-001, NE_11 in HE-CF-NASH-001, NE_22 in HE-CF-NASH-001, q_star in HE-CF-NASH-001, u_22 in HE-CF-NASH-001, W_22 in HE-CF-NASH-001, W_11 in HE-CF-NASH-001, W_12 in HE-CF-NASH-001, W_21 in HE-CF-NASH-001, U_A in HE-CF-NASH-001, U_B in HE-CF-NASH-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-NASH-001
has source in the module Slantchev BL. Game theory: dominance, Nash equilibrium, symmetry. Computational function for HTMT ratios of every construct pair from an indicator correlation matrix

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000911
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for HTMT ratios of every construct pair from an indicator correlation matrix
has calculator text rbar_ij = (r_i1j1 + r_i1j2 + r_i2j1 + r_i2j2 + r_i3j1 + r_i3j2) / 6; rbar_ii = (r_i1i2 + r_i1i3 + r_i2i3) / 3; rbar_jj = r_j1j2; HTMT_ij = rbar_ij / sqrt(rbar_ii * rbar_jj)
has common error note Lower triangle only. Published correlation tables such as Table 5 print only the lower triangle. Pasted as it stands, the OFFSET blocks find nothing above the diagonal, so every ratio with StartI below StartJ returns #DIV/0! (0 if the empty cells hold zeros), because AVERAGE has no numbers in the block between the constructs, and every ratio the other way round is exactly doubled, because SUM skips the empty cells and both within-construct means are halved (derived here). For customer expectations and perceived value the result is #DIV/0! (or 0) or 1.0652 against 0.5326 (computed here). Filling the upper triangle by symmetry first avoids it
has description in the module Computes the heterotrait-monotrait ratio of Eq. 6 of Henseler, Ringle and Sarstedt for every pair of constructs from a full indicator correlation matrix with any number of indicators per construct, and flags the pairs above 0.85 and above 0.90. For each pair it divides the mean of the block of correlations between the two constructs' indicators by the geometric mean of the two within-construct means of the off-diagonal correlations; the two-indicator case is HE-FM-DISV-001. The site calculator has no matrices, so the Math / Code line writes the case of three indicators against two term by term, and the loops over pairs are in the Excel, R and Python code. The bootstrap test of the ratio against 1 that the authors also propose needs respondent-level data and is not implemented here.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-DISV-001, r implementation of HE-CF-DISV-001, python implementation of HE-CF-DISV-001
has mathematical variable HTMT_ij in HE-CF-DISV-001, R in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i1i3 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i2i3 in HE-CF-DISV-001, rbar_ii in HE-CF-DISV-001, rbar_jj in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i1j1 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i1j2 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i2j1 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i2j2 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i3j1 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i3j2 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_i1i2 in HE-CF-DISV-001, r_j1j2 in HE-CF-DISV-001, rbar_ij in HE-CF-DISV-001

has calculator text Delta_E = E_1 - E_0; Delta_C = C_1 - C_0; R = Delta_C / Delta_E; V_E = s_E1^2 / n_1 + s_E0^2 / n_0; V_C = s_C1^2 / n_1 + s_C0^2 / n_0; Cov_EC = r_1 * s_E1 * s_C1 / n_1 + r_0 * s_E0 * s_C0 / n_0; INMB = lambda * Delta_E - Delta_C; SE_INMB = sqrt(lambda^2 * V_E + V_C - 2 * lambda * Cov_EC); M_L = INMB - z * SE_INMB; M_U = INMB + z * SE_INMB; INHB = INMB / lambda; SE_INHB = SE_INMB / lambda; H_L = INHB - z * SE_INHB; H_U = INHB + z * SE_INHB; z_NB = INMB / SE_INMB
has common error note Covariance of the differences set to zero. At GBP 35,000 per QALY, dropping Cov_EC raises SE_INMB from about GBP 979 to GBP 1,059 and moves the interval to about minus GBP 77 to GBP 4,077, which now includes zero, with the probability falling to about 0.970 (computed here for illustration). Resampling or summarising costs and effects separately loses the within-arm correlation that the trial data carry
has description in the module Takes the per-arm summaries a trial report gives, the mean effect and cost in each arm, their standard deviations, the within-arm correlation and the arm size, together with a threshold, and returns incremental net benefit on the money and health scales with its standard error, interval and z statistic, plus the ICER. It first builds the variances and covariance of the two mean differences from the arm summaries, the inputs that HE-FM-AN-004 and HE-CF-AN-001 start from, then forms incremental net monetary benefit and divides by lambda to return the net health benefit of HE-FM-NBA-001, Stinnett and Mullahy's equation 7. The ICER is returned for reading with the quadrant rule of HE-FM-NBA-002, and the Excel, R and Python versions add the probability that net benefit is positive.

Computational function for MCDA totals, ranking and a health gain weight scan for three technologies

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000935
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for MCDA totals, ranking and a health gain weight scan for three technologies
has calculator text v_A = 100 * (x_A - x_worst) / (x_best - x_worst); v_B = 100 * (x_B - x_worst) / (x_best - x_worst); v_C = 100 * (x_C - x_worst) / (x_best - x_worst); w_1 = p_1 / (p_1 + p_2 + p_3); w_2 = p_2 / (p_1 + p_2 + p_3); w_3 = p_3 / (p_1 + p_2 + p_3); V_A = w_1 * v_A + w_2 * s_A + w_3 * e_A; V_B = w_1 * v_B + w_2 * s_B + w_3 * e_B; V_C = w_1 * v_C + w_2 * s_C + w_3 * e_C; V_top = max(max(V_A, V_B), V_C)
has common error note Ranking at the elicited weights only. Reporting only the ranking at the elicited health gain weight of 0.50 hides that C, with no health gain, ranks first at every health gain weight below 2/7, about 0.29 (derived from the article's example). The second ISPOR report states that, at a minimum, deterministic analysis should be undertaken
has description in the module Takes the health gain of three technologies with the fixed range of that criterion, their scores on two further criteria and the swing points of the three criteria, and returns each technology's health gain score by HE-FM-MCDH-001, the weights and overall values by HE-FM-MCDH-002, and the highest overall value. Its inputs are raw performance on the first criterion rather than the partial values of HE-FM-MCDH-002. The R and Python code also rank the technologies, scan the health gain weight on a grid of 101 values, 0, 0.01 and so on up to 1, with the other two weights kept in the ratio of their points, and return, for each pair of technologies, the weight at which their overall values are equal. Each total is linear in that weight, so this weight takes the form of the switching weight w_s of HE-FM-WE-004, which that record computes only for the QALYs of two groups, here with the other-criteria blend in place of group A QALYs and the health gain score less that blend in place of group B QALYs (derived). It marks a change of ranking only when it is at least 0 and at most 1; a value below 0 or above 1 means the pair keeps the same order at every feasible weight, and a pair with equal slopes either never ties or, with equal totals, ties at every weight (derived). Equal overall values are reported as ties, with no rule to break them.

Computational function for one tender under both pricing rules and a seeded check of the uniform-cost closed forms

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000960
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for one tender under both pricing rules and a seeded check of the uniform-cost closed forms
has calculator text b_1 = c_1 + (c_high - c_1) / N; b_2 = c_2 + (c_high - c_2) / N; b_3 = c_3 + (c_high - c_3) / N; p_FP = min(min(b_1, b_2), b_3); p_SP = max(min(c_1, c_2), min(max(c_1, c_2), c_3)); d = p_FP - p_SP
has common error note fresh random draws in the check. Using live RAND() in the draw table changes the simulated means at every recalculation, and with a 4 standard error band a correct sheet then fails about 6 times in 100,000 recalculations (normal approximation, derived), so the table is pasted as values or the seed is fixed in R and Python
has description in the module Runs one tender under both pricing rules and checks the closed forms of HE-FM-AUCT-001 and HE-FM-AUCT-002 by simulating many tenders with a fixed seed. In one tender the second-price rule pays the winner the second-lowest cost, since every supplier bids its cost, and the first-price rule pays the lowest equilibrium bid. The lowest of the bids is the min form used for observed prices in HE-FM-BOC-003, and the scalar line writes the second-lowest of three costs as the median of three. The simulation draws N uniform costs per tender, sorts them, and averages the lowest cost, the second-lowest cost and the winning first-price bid, which converge to E_c1, E_p and E_b.

Computational function for per-person and population EVSI of a two-arm trial under normal incremental net benefit

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000843
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for per-person and population EVSI of a two-arm trial under normal incremental net benefit
has calculator text v0 = sd0^2; sigma2 = sd_A^2 + sd_B^2; n0 = sigma2 / v0; nu = n / (n + n0); s2 = nu * v0; s = sqrt(s2); z_s = abs(m0) / s; phi_s = exp(-z_s * z_s / 2) / sqrt(2 * 3.14159265); EVSI = s * (phi_s - z_s * Q_s); z_0 = abs(m0) / sd0; phi_0 = exp(-z_0 * z_0 / 2) / sqrt(2 * 3.14159265); EVPI = sd0 * (phi_0 - z_0 * Q_0); share = EVSI / EVPI; D = (1 + r) * (1 - (1 + r)^(-Y)) / r; EVSI_pop = EVSI * N * D
has common error note Total trial size entered as the number per arm. Entering a trial of 200 patients in total as n of 200 per arm doubles the information and gives about GBP 99.82 per patient instead of GBP 61.68 for 100 per arm
has description in the module Takes the inputs a trial planner usually holds, the prior mean and standard deviation of incremental net benefit from the probabilistic analysis, the per-patient standard deviation of net benefit in each arm, the number of patients per arm and the size and lifetime of the beneficiary population, and returns per-person EVSI, per-person EVPI under normality, the share of EVPI the trial captures and population EVSI. It chains HE-FM-EVSI-002 (preposterior variance), HE-FM-EVSI-003 (closed-form EVSI, applied also at the prior variance for EVPI) and HE-FM-EVSI-004 (population EVSI), so its inputs differ from the symbols of any one formula. The calculator version takes the two upper-tail probabilities Q_s and Q_0 as inputs because it has no normal distribution function, and the Excel, R and Python versions compute them. The same EVSI can be checked by the single-loop Monte Carlo scheme of the second ISPOR report, drawing a true incremental net benefit from the prior and a trial estimate around it, and averaging the amount by which the posterior mean falls on the other side of zero from the prior mean, the value of switching in those results.

Computational function for R_0 as the spectral radius of a next-generation matrix of any size

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000863
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for R_0 as the spectral radius of a next-generation matrix of any size
has calculator text a_1 = k_11 + k_12; a_2 = k_21 + k_22; b_1 = k_11 * a_1 + k_12 * a_2; b_2 = k_21 * a_1 + k_22 * a_2; c_1 = k_11 * b_1 + k_12 * b_2; c_2 = k_21 * b_1 + k_22 * b_2; R_pow = (c_1 + c_2) / (b_1 + b_2); R_two = sqrt((c_1 + c_2) / (a_1 + a_2)); T_K = k_11 + k_22; D_K = k_11 * k_22 - k_12 * k_21; R_0 = (T_K + sqrt(T_K^2 - 4 * D_K)) / 2
has common error note One-generation ratio on a host and vector matrix. Reading the ratio of successive generation totals as R_0 for the illustrative host and vector matrix gives 2.25 and then about 0.889, never settling, while R_0 is about 1.414; the two-generation ratio or iteration on K plus the identity removes the swing
Spectral bound of F minus V taken as R_0. The largest eigenvalue of F minus V, the Jacobian of the infected compartments, has the same sign as R_0 minus 1 by Theorem 1 of van den Driessche, but it is a growth rate. For the article's SIR infection it is 0.30 minus 0.10, or 0.2 per day, the early growth rate r, not R_0 of 3
has description in the module Takes a next-generation matrix K with n groups, or the matrices F and V of a compartmental model from which K is F times the inverse of V, and returns R_0 as the spectral radius of K together with the dominant eigenvector scaled to sum to 1, the share of cases in each group once the generations settle. R and Python call an eigenvalue routine. Excel has no eigenvalue function, so it uses power iteration with helper columns, applying K to a vector of cases generation by generation, the growth factor per generation that Diekmann, Heesterbeek and Roberts take as the characterisation of R_0. The calculator line shows the two-group case over three generations started from one case in each group, beside the closed form of HE-FM-BRN-005, and the inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables, which cover two groups only.

Computational function for ranking allocations by utilitarian, isoelastic and maximin health social welfare

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000857
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for ranking allocations by utilitarian, isoelastic and maximin health social welfare
has calculator text U_P = h_P1 + h_P2 + h_P3; U_T = h_T1 + h_T2 + h_T3; U_R = h_R1 + h_R2 + h_R3; W_P = (h_P1^(1 - epsilon) + h_P2^(1 - epsilon) + h_P3^(1 - epsilon)) / (1 - epsilon); W_T = (h_T1^(1 - epsilon) + h_T2^(1 - epsilon) + h_T3^(1 - epsilon)) / (1 - epsilon); W_R = (h_R1^(1 - epsilon) + h_R2^(1 - epsilon) + h_R3^(1 - epsilon)) / (1 - epsilon); M_P = min(h_P1, min(h_P2, h_P3)); M_T = min(h_T1, min(h_T2, h_T3)); M_R = min(h_R1, min(h_R2, h_R3))
has common error note Isoelastic values that underflow at very high epsilon. At epsilon of 200 each term h_i^(1-epsilon) is far below the smallest double-precision number, so W is zero for all three options and they appear tied. The log-scale key used here still ranks R first, as the maximin form does
has description in the module Takes the health of each equal-sized group under each option and an inequality aversion parameter, and returns for every option the utilitarian total, the isoelastic welfare of HE-FM-SWF-001 (HE-FM-SWF-002 at epsilon of 1) and the maximin welfare of HE-FM-SWF-003, with the option ranked first under each. It repeats the article's three-step ranking for any number of options and groups, and it ranks the isoelastic values on a log scale so that very high epsilon values do not underflow. The Math / Code line shows three options and three groups at one epsilon other than 1, and the R and Python versions take any number. The values of epsilon at which two options swap rank belong to the Distributional Cost-Effectiveness package and are not computed here.

has description in the module Takes a probabilistic sample as an analyst holds it, a table of N runs with the sampled values of the parameters of interest and the net benefit of each of D options, fits a least squares regression of each option's net benefit less the baseline's on the chosen parameters, and returns the EVPPI from the fitted values, the EVPI from the raw net benefits and a flag that the estimate lies within its bounds. It chains HE-FM-EVPP-003 with HE-FM-VOI-001 for any number of options, so the inputs differ from the formula's variables, which start from fitted values for three options. A linear model in indicator, polynomial or product terms stands in here for the generalised additive model of Strong, Oakley and Brennan. The Math / Code line takes two options and four runs, with the fitted values from the regression entered as inputs, and the R and Python versions fit the regression for any number of runs and options.

Computational function for Sullivan active life expectancy with its standard error over any number of age bands

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000963
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for Sullivan active life expectancy with its standard error over any number of age bands
has calculator text w_1 = L_1 / l_x; w_2 = L_2 / l_x; w_3 = L_3 / l_x; ALE_x = (1 - pi_1) * w_1 + (1 - pi_2) * w_2 + (1 - pi_3) * w_3; V_ALE = w_1^2 * pi_1 * (1 - pi_1) / N_1 + w_2^2 * pi_2 * (1 - pi_2) / N_2 + w_3^2 * pi_3 * (1 - pi_3) / N_3; SE_ALE = sqrt(V_ALE); lo_ALE = ALE_x - 1.96 * SE_ALE; hi_ALE = ALE_x + 1.96 * SE_ALE
has common error note Reading the interval as covering all uncertainty. The interval reflects survey sampling only. Imai and Soneji's Table 2 shows that allowing for sampling variability in the death rates widens the interval at age 20 for the 1999 United States population, 54.40 to 54.53 years with the death rates known and 54.37 to 54.56 with them estimated; misclassification, a survey that leaves out care homes (HE-ER-ALE-004) and departures from stationarity are not in the standard error at all
Weights left unsquared in the variance. Writing the variance as the sum of w_i pi_i (1 minus pi_i) over N_i gives 0.0022 in place of 0.0117 on the baseline, a standard error of 0.0464 in place of 0.1083 and an interval less than half as wide (derived). The weight enters the variance squared because each band's prevalence is multiplied by L_i over l_x
has description in the module Takes a period life table's survivors at the starting age and person-years by age band, the dependency prevalence measured in each band and the number of survey respondents behind each prevalence, and returns active life expectancy, life expectancy, expected dependent years, the active share, the standard error of active life expectancy and a normal 95% interval, for any number of bands. The point estimate is HE-FM-ALE-001. The variance is the standard estimator Imai and Soneji print as their equation 13, the sum over bands of pi_i (1 minus pi_i) L_i squared over N_i, divided by l_x squared; each band's term takes the form of the squared standard error of HE-FM-BINOM-004, with survey respondents in place of that record's patients, times the squared weight L_i over l_x (derived), so the bands are treated as independent binomial samples and the survey respondents N_i are inputs the formula does not hold. For the 1999 United States population with an activities of daily living definition, Imai and Soneji report 14.31 years at 65 with a 95% interval of 14.24 to 14.37 when the death rates are treated as known. The calculator line writes three bands out term by term; the loops over bands are in the Excel, R and Python code.

has calculator text x_peak = 1 / beta; W_lo = C * x_lo * exp(-beta * x_lo); W_hi = C * x_hi * exp(-beta * x_hi)
has common error note Span read as the ages whose deaths gain from weighting. The ages 8.5 to 55.5 are where a year lived is weighted above 1, not the ages at death whose loss rises. Barendregt and colleagues show that, because years of life lost already favour the young, the ages that gain from weighting are 0 to 27, and in the article's illustration a death at 40, inside the span, loses 8% under age weighting
has description in the module Takes the constants C and beta and a level, by default 1, and returns the two ages at which the weight equals that level, one on each side of the peak 1/beta, so the span of ages weighted above uniform can be read off. The crossing ages have no closed form in elementary functions, so the code finds them numerically by root search on each side of the peak. The calculator line checks a pair of ages by returning their weights. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables, which take one age.

has common error note Enumerating a large candidate list. Crown and colleagues name branch-and-bound as an exact solution algorithm for mixed-integer models and describe heuristics that give a good but not necessarily optimal solution in reasonable time
has description in the module Takes the names, budget costs and QALY gains of any number of independent programmes, a budget and the names of any programmes a rule requires to be funded, checks every one of the 2^n packages of whole programmes, and returns the best package that fits the budget and includes the required programmes, with its cost, its QALYs and the number of packages that qualify. Run once without and once with the required programmes, it gives the health forgone to the rule, the explicit cost of equity that Stinnett and Paltiel describe and the opportunity cost of an equity constraint that Epstein and colleagues estimate. The inputs are lists of programmes, not the scalar inputs of HE-FM-IPH-001. The Math / Code line shows four programmes with programme 4 required, and the R and Python versions take any number of programmes and any required set.

Computational function for the cost-effectiveness frontier by removing dominated and extendedly dominated strategies

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000851
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for the cost-effectiveness frontier by removing dominated and extendedly dominated strategies
has calculator text r_2 = (C_2 - C_1) / (Q_2 - Q_1); r_3 = (C_3 - C_2) / (Q_3 - Q_2); r_4 = (C_4 - C_3) / (Q_4 - Q_3); exd_2 = r_2 > r_3; exd_3 = r_3 > r_4; r_42 = (C_4 - C_2) / (Q_4 - Q_2)
has common error note Testing extended dominance before strict dominance. Left in the list, R2 gives a ratio of minus GBP 75,000 per QALY against R1, and the removal rule then drops R1, R2 and R3 in turn. The frontier becomes R0, R4 and R5 with ratios of about GBP 26,667 and 60,000, so at GBP 25,000 the choice is R0 and GBP 500 per person of net monetary benefit is lost against R1 (computed here for illustration)
has description in the module Takes the names, expected costs and expected QALYs of any number of mutually exclusive strategies and returns the strategies on the cost-effectiveness frontier in cost order with their sequential ICERs, by the conventional elimination that Suen and Goldhaber-Fiebert summarise. It sorts by cost, removes dominated strategies, ties included, with the rule of HE-FM-DOM-002, computes the ratio of each strategy against the one before it, removes the first strategy whose ratio is higher than the next one, as in HE-FM-EXD-001, and recomputes until the ratios rise. The finished ratios are the slopes that HE-FM-CEF-001 reads. The inputs are lists of strategies, not the three named strategies of the formula. The Math / Code line shows one pass for four strategies already in cost order with no strict dominance, and the R and Python versions take any number and repeat the passes.

Computational function for the critical inequality aversion at which two options give equal EDE health

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000855
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for the critical inequality aversion at which two options give equal EDE health
has calculator text E_A = (s_1 * hA_1^(1 - epsilon) + s_2 * hA_2^(1 - epsilon))^(1 / (1 - epsilon)); E_B = (s_1 * hB_1^(1 - epsilon) + s_2 * hB_2^(1 - epsilon))^(1 / (1 - epsilon)); D = E_A - E_B; D_pop = N_pop * D
has common error note Searching a bracket in which the difference does not change sign. With the upper end set to 10 for the article's equal-sharing case, the difference is positive at both ends, about 600 and 71.4 EDE QALYs, and a search that skips the end check returns 10 as if it were the switching value. The function returns NA in that case, and the bracket is widened
has description in the module Takes the population share and the health per person of each social group under two options, such as a programme and its comparator from HE-FM-DCEA-001, and a bracket of inequality aversion values, and returns by bisection the Atkinson epsilon at which the two options give equal EDE health, the point where the change in EDE health of HE-FM-DCEA-002 changes sign. At each step it evaluates the share-weighted EDE of HE-CF-ATK-001 for both options at the midpoint of the bracket, keeps the half in which the difference changes sign and stops when the bracket is narrower than the tolerance, for any number of groups. The two-group closed approximation in the equity-efficiency trade-off package gives about 11.77 for the article's example, while solving the EDE comparison gives about 11.76, the value the article reports. The Math / Code line evaluates the difference for two groups at a given epsilon, the quantity bisection drives to zero, and the Excel, R and Python versions run the search.

Computational function for the one-pass net monetary benefit search for the cost-effectiveness frontier

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000852
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for the one-pass net monetary benefit search for the cost-effectiveness frontier
has calculator text N_1 = lambda * Q_1 - C_1; N_2 = lambda * Q_2 - C_2; N_3 = lambda * Q_3 - C_3; N_4 = lambda * Q_4 - C_4; N_max = max(N_1, N_2, N_3, N_4); L_1 = N_max - N_1; L_2 = N_max - N_2; L_3 = N_max - N_3; L_4 = N_max - N_4
has common error note A coarse grid of round values. For options A to D, B is chosen only for values per QALY at or above GBP 10,000 and at or below GBP 18,000. A grid of 0, 20,000 and 40,000 returns A and D only, so B drops out of the frontier and the choice at GBP 15,000 is misread as A, which gives up GBP 1,500 per person against B (computed here for illustration). Suen and Goldhaber-Fiebert include every positive pairwise ICER in the grid to prevent this
has description in the module Takes the names, expected costs and expected QALYs of any number of strategies and a largest value per QALY of interest, and returns the frontier strategies by the one-pass method of Suen and Goldhaber-Fiebert. It calculates net monetary benefit (HE-FM-NMB-001) for every strategy at every value in a grid made of zero, every positive pairwise ICER up to the largest value and that value itself, and keeps the strategies that come out on top somewhere in the grid, with no rounds of elimination. At one value it also returns the net monetary benefit each strategy gives up, L_k of HE-FM-CEF-002. The Math / Code line shows four strategies at one value, and the R and Python versions take any number of strategies and the whole grid.

Computational function for the second-best menu against shut-down and complete information with an outside option

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000936
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for the second-best menu against shut-down and complete information with an outside option
has calculator text q_L = (a - theta_L) / b; q_FB = (a - theta_H) / b; theta_v = theta_H + nu / (1 - nu) * (theta_H - theta_L); q_SB = max(0, (a - theta_v) / b); S_L = a * q_L - b * q_L^2 / 2; S_FB = a * q_FB - b * q_FB^2 / 2; S_SB = a * q_SB - b * q_SB^2 / 2; V_FB = nu * (S_L - theta_L * q_L - U_0) + (1 - nu) * (S_FB - theta_H * q_FB - U_0); V_SB = nu * (S_L - theta_L * q_L - U_0 - (theta_H - theta_L) * q_SB) + (1 - nu) * (S_SB - theta_H * q_SB - U_0); V_shut = nu * (S_L - theta_L * q_L - U_0); V_star = max(V_SB, V_shut); shut = (V_shut >= V_SB); loss = V_FB - V_star
has common error note Outside option ignored. With a zero status quo and a positive solution of (2.28), serving the high-cost type is always better here, because S(q) less S'(q) q equals b q^2 / 2, which is above zero (derived, the argument of the remark in section 2.7.3 of Laffont and Martimort). Leaving U_0 out therefore always keeps the high-cost type in. With U_0 = 200 the menu of HE-EX-MECH-004 leaves the high-cost type 0 and the low-cost type 80 (GBP thousand), both below the outside option, so neither type accepts and nothing is bought (computed here)
has description in the module Compares three policies for a purchaser buying from a supplier whose unit cost is theta_L with probability nu or theta_H otherwise, when both types have an outside option U_0 at least zero. Under complete information each type produces its first-best output and is paid its cost plus U_0. The second-best menu of HE-FM-MECH-002 pays the high-cost type its cost plus U_0 and the low-cost type its cost plus U_0 plus the rent (theta_H minus theta_L) q_H. Shutting out the high-cost type serves only the low-cost type at first-best output with no information rent. The purchaser takes the better of the two incentive-feasible policies, which is (2.32) of Laffont and Martimort, and the gap to the complete-information payoff measures what the hidden cost costs the purchaser (derived). The second-best output does not depend on U_0, so a large outside option makes shut-down optimal even when (2.28) has a positive solution.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-MECH-001, r implementation of HE-CF-MECH-001, python implementation of HE-CF-MECH-001
