Computational function for the stop or continue decision at one interim look of a two-arm trial

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000860
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Computational function for the stop or continue decision at one interim look of a two-arm trial
has calculator text n_t = n_0 + m_1; mu_t = (n_0 * mu_0 + m_1 * xbar_1) / n_t; sd_t = sigma_X / sqrt(n_t); s_m = sigma_X * sqrt(m / (n_t * (n_t + m))); z = abs(mu_t) / s_m; phi_z = exp(-z * z / 2) / sqrt(2 * 3.14159265); EVSI = s_m * (phi_z - z * Q_z); EVSI_pop = P * EVSI; G_off = EVSI_pop - m * c; NG = EVSI_pop - m * c - C_a - m * abs(mu_t); go = NG > 0
has common error note Interim effective sample size left at the prior value. Keeping n_0 of 25 for the preposterior variance at the favourable interim look prices the remaining 100 pairs at about GBP 12.36 million instead of GBP 3.02 million and the net gain at about GBP 11.94 million instead of GBP 2.60 million, about four times the value of the remaining pairs, because the 100 pairs already observed are ignored (computed here for illustration)
Signed interim mean used for the participants' loss. At a posterior mean of minus GBP 1,500 the signed term adds GBP 150,000 to the gain instead of charging it, giving about minus GBP 227,228 instead of minus GBP 527,228, as if the participants on the new technology gained from it (computed here for illustration)
has description in the module Takes the design-stage prior for incremental net monetary benefit, the pairs observed and their mean at the interim look, the remaining pairs, the sampling standard deviation per pair, the patients informed and the costs, and returns the interim posterior, the per-patient and population EVSI of the remaining pairs, the offline gain of HE-FM-AVOI-001 with no switching cost, the net gain with the participants' loss and the decision. It chains the normal update of HE-FM-BAD-001 in effective sample size form, the preposterior variance of HE-FM-EVSI-002 with n_t in place of n0, the closed-form EVSI of HE-FM-EVSI-003 at the interim mean, HE-FM-AVOI-001, and HE-FM-ENBS-001 with the cost of HE-FM-ENBS-002 for the remaining pairs (n equal to 2m, a variable cost of c/2 per participant, a share of one half on the arm expected to be inferior and the analysis cost C_a in place of the fixed cost), so its inputs differ from the symbols of any one formula. The calculator takes Q_z from 1-NORM.S.DIST(z,TRUE) because it has no normal distribution function. The Excel, R and Python versions compute it and also scan every remaining size up to the maximum, continuing if any one-stage plan has a positive net gain, the KG* rule of Chick and colleagues.

has description in the module Takes the GPs or other capacity of any number of provider locations, the populations of any number of residential locations, the matrix of travel times between them and a threshold, and returns each provider's supply ratio, each location's 2SFCA score, the population-weighted mean of the scores and their population-weighted variance, scaled to a stated number of people. It applies HE-FM-ACCS-001 to every provider and location at once, with SUMPRODUCT over a travel-time block in Excel and matrix operations in R, so its inputs are a matrix and two lists rather than the formula's named scalars. The mean is the check that the scores conserve total supply, and the variance, the population-weighted second moment that HE-VR-HINQ-007 takes over ranks, here taken over scores, is Wang 2012's equation 4, the inequality objective of Wang and Tang, divided by the total population (derived). The calculator line repeats the chain for the article's two practices and three neighbourhoods and adds the mean and variance; any other size is in the Excel, R and Python code.

has common error note Reading a positive pooled result as a gain in every group. Copas selection parameters and implied unpublished studies from two assumed publication probabilities

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000909
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Copas selection parameters and implied unpublished studies from two assumed publication probabilities
has calculator text gamma_1 = (z_a - z_b) / (1 / s_a - 1 / s_b); gamma_0 = z_b - gamma_1 / s_b; M = n_a * (1 - P_a) / P_a + n_b * (1 - P_b) / P_b
has common error note Standard errors in place of precisions. Dividing the difference in quantiles by s_a minus s_b, which is -0.30, instead of 1 / s_a minus 1 / s_b, which is 7.5, gives gamma_1 = -6.767 in the moderate scenario, a selection equation in which the most precise studies are the least likely to be published (computed here for illustration)
has description in the module Takes the publication probabilities assumed for studies at two standard errors, the way the article sets each scenario, solves the selection equation of HE-FM-COPAS-001 at both for gamma_1 and gamma_0, and returns them with the implied number of unpublished studies of HE-FM-COPAS-002 for the published studies at those standard errors. With quantiles z_a = Phi^-1(P_a) and z_b = Phi^-1(P_b), gamma_1 = (z_a minus z_b) / (1 / s_a minus 1 / s_b) and gamma_0 = z_b minus gamma_1 / s_b. Huang and colleagues set the selection parameters of their simulation the same way, by solving two publication probability equations for studies of 20 and 500 patients in a selection equation written on the square root of the sample size; the closed form above, for the equation in 1 / s, is derived. The copas function of metasens chooses its grid of gamma_0 and gamma_1 to cover a range of publication probabilities for the study with the largest standard error, and repeating the calculation over pairs of probabilities builds the selection columns of the article's scenario table. The adjusted effect at each pair needs the conditional maximum likelihood fit of the copas software and is not computed here.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-COPAS-001, r implementation of HE-CF-COPAS-001, python implementation of HE-CF-COPAS-001
has mathematical variable gamma_1 in HE-CF-COPAS-001, gamma_0 in HE-CF-COPAS-001, P_a in HE-CF-COPAS-001, P_b in HE-CF-COPAS-001, n_a in HE-CF-COPAS-001, n_b in HE-CF-COPAS-001, M in HE-CF-COPAS-001, s_a in HE-CF-COPAS-001, s_b in HE-CF-COPAS-001, z_a in HE-CF-COPAS-001, z_b in HE-CF-COPAS-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-COPAS-001
has source in the module Huang A, Komukai S, Friede T, Hattori S. Using clinical trial registries to inform Copas selection model for publication bias in meta-analysis. Delta-method, Fieller and net benefit intervals from trial summary statistics

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000754
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Delta-method, Fieller and net benefit intervals from trial summary statistics
has calculator text R = Delta_C / Delta_E; SE_R = abs(R) * sqrt(SE_C^2 / Delta_C^2 + SE_E^2 / Delta_E^2 - 2 * Cov_CE / (Delta_C * Delta_E)); D_L = R - z * SE_R; D_U = R + z * SE_R; a = Delta_E^2 - z^2 * SE_E^2; b = -2 * (Delta_C * Delta_E - z^2 * Cov_CE); c = Delta_C^2 - z^2 * SE_C^2; F_L = (-b - sqrt(b^2 - 4 * a * c)) / (2 * a); F_U = (-b + sqrt(b^2 - 4 * a * c)) / (2 * a); INB = lambda * Delta_E - Delta_C; SE_INB = sqrt(lambda^2 * SE_E^2 + SE_C^2 - 2 * lambda * Cov_CE); N_L = INB - z * SE_INB; N_U = INB + z * SE_INB; z_INB = INB / SE_INB
has common error note Passing patient-level standard deviations instead of standard errors. Entering the per-patient standard deviations of £6,708 and 0.447 as SE_C and SE_E makes a and the discriminant negative, so Fieller's set becomes the whole real line, and the net benefit interval at £30,000 per QALY widens to about -£28,389 to £30,389. The function needs the standard errors of the two mean differences
has description in the module Takes the summary statistics a trial-based analysis produces, the incremental mean cost and effect, their standard errors and covariance, and a threshold, and returns three large-sample intervals in one pass: the delta-method interval for the ICER (HE-FM-AN-002), Fieller's interval (HE-FM-AN-003) and the incremental net benefit interval with the probability that net benefit is positive (HE-FM-AN-004). The delta-method and net benefit intervals are Wald intervals (HE-FM-AN-001) applied to the ratio and to a linear combination of the differences; Fieller's method applies the normal approximation to the two differences themselves. The procedure branches on the sign of the Fieller coefficient a, so it reports whether Fieller's set is a bounded interval, two unbounded pieces or the whole real line, rather than returning two roots that may bound nothing.

DerSimonian and Laird meta-analysis from study estimates and standard errors

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000759
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of DerSimonian and Laird meta-analysis from study estimates and standard errors
has calculator text v = se^2; w = 1 / v; theta_F = sum(w * y) / sum(w); SE_F = sqrt(1 / sum(w)); Q = sum(w * (y - theta_F)^2); I2 = max(0, (Q - (k - 1)) / Q) * 100; tau2 = max(0, (Q - (k - 1)) / (sum(w) - sum(w^2) / sum(w))); theta_R = sum(y / (v + tau2)) / sum(1 / (v + tau2)); SE_R = sqrt(1 / sum(1 / (v + tau2))); CI_low_R = theta_R - 1.96 * SE_R; CI_high_R = theta_R + 1.96 * SE_R
has common error note Entering hazard ratios instead of log hazard ratios. Entering the hazard ratios 0.549, 0.951 and 0.670 with the log-scale standard errors mixes two scales. In the three-trial example the function then returns I-squared of about 47% instead of 70.9% and tau2 of about 0.028 instead of 0.07625, and exponentiating the pooled value of about 0.848 suggests a hazard ratio of about 2.3, that is harm. Ratio measures enter as natural logarithms and the outputs are exponentiated afterwards
has description in the module Takes the two columns an aggregate data synthesis starts from, each study's effect estimate and its standard error, and returns the full DerSimonian and Laird analysis in one pass: the common-effect estimate and standard error (HE-FM-ADMA-001), Cochran's Q (HE-FM-ADMA-002), I-squared (HE-FM-ADMA-003), tau2 (HE-FM-ADMA-004) and the random-effects mean, standard error and 95% confidence interval (HE-FM-ADMA-005). The inputs differ from the formulae's variables: the formulae take variances and previously computed statistics, while the function squares the standard errors itself and each step uses the vector or statistic produced by the step before.

Discounted life years and QALYs from a mixture cure model by numerical integration

has calculator text QALY_d = sum_(i=1)^n [dt * exp(-(mu + log(1 + d)) * t_i) * (pi * u_C + (1 - pi) * u_U * exp(-(t_i / b)^k))]; t_i = (i - 0.5) * dt; n = T_h / dt
has common error note Horizon too short for cured patients. A 40-year horizon leaves cured patients alive: with utilities of 1 and no discounting the function returns about 6.49 years instead of 7.75 (computed here for illustration)
Negating before the power in Excel. Excel applies the minus sign before ^, so EXP(-(t/WeibScale)^WeibShape) raises minus t/b to the power k. It returns #NUM! for a shape of 1.5 and the wrong sign for a shape of 2. The power needs its own brackets, as in EXP(-((t/WeibScale)^WeibShape))
has description in the module Takes the inputs a cure model analysis usually holds, a cure fraction, a background hazard, a Weibull curve for the excess survival of the uncured, utilities by cure status, an annual discount rate and a horizon, and returns discounted QALYs by summing the mixture cure curve HE-FM-CRM-001, weighted by utility and the continuous discount factor, over a fine time grid with the midpoint rule. Othus and colleagues computed the mean survival of uncured patients, the integral of S_B(t) times S_E(t), by numerical integration. The inputs therefore differ from the closed forms HE-FM-CRM-003 and HE-FM-CRM-004: the excess hazard need not be constant, and the function builds the areas E_B and E_U itself. With both utilities set to 1 and d to 0 it returns T_bar.

Exact binomial probability, cumulative probability and percentiles of an event count

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000785
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Exact binomial probability, cumulative probability and percentiles of an event count
has calculator text P_r = C(n, r) * p^r * (1 - p)^(n - r); F_r = sum_(j=0)^r [C(n, j) * p^j * (1 - p)^(n - j)]; q_lo = min(x : F_x >= a_lo); q_hi = min(x : F_x >= a_hi)
has common error note Percentiles of a simulated count read as uncertainty about p. The range of 128 to 172 events per 1,000 patients is stochastic noise at a fixed probability of 0.15 and shrinks, relative to the mean, as more patients are simulated. Uncertainty in the probability from the 40-patient study is far wider, about 72 to 292 events per 1,000 from Beta(7, 35)
has description in the module Takes the inputs a model or a study summary usually holds, the number of patients, the event probability, a count and two percentile levels, and returns the exact probability of the count, the cumulative probability of at most that count and two percentiles of the count. The cumulative probability sums the probability function HE-FM-BINOM-001 over every count up to r, and each percentile is found by searching for the smallest count whose cumulative probability reaches the level, because the binomial percent point function has no closed form. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the levels a_lo and a_hi are extra, and the outputs include percentiles that the formula cannot give.

Exact Hedges' correction factor by the log-gamma function with g and its variance

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000877
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Exact Hedges' correction factor by the log-gamma function with g and its variance
has calculator text m = n_1 + n_2 - 2; J = 1 - 3 / (4 * m - 1); g = J * d; V_d = (n_1 + n_2) / (n_1 * n_2) + d^2 / (2 * (n_1 + n_2)); V_g = J^2 * V_d; SE_g = sqrt(V_g)
has common error note Gamma function overflow. Computing the ratio with the gamma function itself fails in large trials, because the gamma function exceeds the largest double-precision number above an argument of about 171.6. In Python `math.gamma` raises an overflow error and in R `gamma` returns Inf, so the factor is Inf at 344 degrees of freedom, a total of 346 patients, and NaN from 345 (checked here), while the log-gamma form returns about 0.997818. Excel's GAMMALN avoids the problem in the same way
has description in the module Computes the exact correction factor of Hedges, the ratio of gamma functions in his equation 6e, through the logarithm of the gamma function so that it does not overflow in large samples, and returns with it Hedges' g, the approximate factor of HE-FM-HEDG-001 and its error, the variance and standard error of g of HE-FM-HEDG-002 with the exact factor, and the exact variance of the corrected estimate from Hedges' Theorem 2 evaluated with g in place of delta. The site calculator has no gamma function, so the Math / Code line runs the approximate chain of HE-FM-HEDG-001 and HE-FM-HEDG-002, and the exact steps are in the Excel, R and Python code. The inputs are d from HE-FM-CD-001 and the two group sizes.

Final attack rate by iteration and infections averted by pre-epidemic vaccination

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000764
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Final attack rate by iteration and infections averted by pre-epidemic vaccination
has calculator text s_0 = 1 - V; z_V = s_0 * (1 - exp(-R_0 * z_V)) solved by iteration from z_V = s_0; I_avert = N_pop * (z_0 - z_V); I_static = N_pop * V * z_0; C_avert = c_v * N_pop * V / I_avert
has common error note Holding the attack rate fixed when coverage changes. A static model that applies z_0 to the unvaccinated counts 23,904 of the 43,910 infections averted in the article's example, about 54%, and gives about £62.75 per infection averted instead of £34.16
Starting the iteration at zero. Starting from z = 0 returns the trivial solution z = 0, which satisfies the relation but is not the final size of the epidemic when R_0 s_0 exceeds 1. The iteration starts from s_0
has description in the module Takes the inputs of a vaccination scenario, a basic reproduction number, the coverage reached before the epidemic, the population size and the cost per person vaccinated, and returns the final attack rate with and without the programme, the infections averted and the cost per infection averted. The final size relation has z on both sides and cannot be rearranged to give z directly, so the function solves it by fixed-point iteration: starting from z = s_0, it repeats the update z = s_0(1 minus exp(minus R_0 z)), which falls steadily to the positive solution whenever R_0 s_0 exceeds 1. The inputs therefore differ from those of HE-FM-ATR-005, which runs the other way, from an observed final attack rate to R_0.

has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-ATR-001, r implementation of HE-CF-ATR-001, python implementation of HE-CF-ATR-001

Grid posterior over the between-study standard deviation in a Bayesian meta-analysis

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000777
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Grid posterior over the between-study standard deviation in a Bayesian meta-analysis
has calculator text tau_j = (j - 0.5) * tmax / J; w_ij = 1 / (s_i^2 + tau_j^2); W_j = sum_i(w_ij); m_j = sum_i(w_ij * y_i) / W_j; lp_j = 0.5 * sum_i(log(w_ij) - w_ij * (y_i - m_j)^2) - 0.5 * log(W_j) + logprior(tau_j); post_j = exp(lp_j - max(lp)) / sum_j(exp(lp_j - max(lp))); tau_med = smallest tau_j with sum_(l<=j)(post_l) >= 0.5; P_mu_neg = sum_j(post_j * Phi(-m_j * sqrt(W_j)))
has common error note Leaving out the change of variable when a tau2 prior is used on a tau grid. The Turner priors are densities for tau2. Using the log-normal term without the leading -log(t) treats the density for tau2 as a density for tau and, for the article's trials, gives a posterior median of tau of about 0.187 instead of 0.133 and a probability of about 0.943 instead of 0.961 that mu is below zero
has description in the module Takes the trial estimates and standard errors of a pairwise meta-analysis and a prior for the between-study standard deviation tau, and returns the posterior median of tau and the posterior probability that the mean effect mu is below zero by numerical integration over a fine grid of tau values. At each grid value it applies HE-FM-BMA-001 to get the conditional posterior mean and precision of mu, weights the grid value by its prior density times the marginal likelihood of the data with mu integrated out, and normalises the weights. The inputs differ from the formula's variables: the formula takes one fixed tau2, while the function takes a prior over tau and a grid and averages over them. This is the numerical integration route that Turner and colleagues describe as an alternative to MCMC for a summary-data meta-analysis.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-BMA-001, r implementation of HE-CF-BMA-001, python implementation of HE-CF-BMA-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-BMA-001
has source in the module Turner RM, Jackson D, Wei Y, Thompson SG, Higgins JPT. Predictive distributions for between-study heterogeneity and simple methods for their application in Bayesian meta-analysis. Statistics in Medicine. 2015;34(6):984-998. Section 2.2, which evaluates the constant of proportionality, posterior moments and cumulative distribution functions of the mean effect and tau2 by numerical integration and finds percentiles with a search algorithm. The vague U(0, 2) prior on tau is from Dias S, Welton NJ, Sutton AJ, Ades AE. NICE DSU TSD 2, section 6.2. https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.6381
has test Four mortality trials under the vague U(0, 2) prior (HE-CF-BMA-001)

At 125% of expected claims the expected payment is about 0.22% of claims for 200 members and about 8.1% for 20 members. has description in the module Takes the inputs an analyst usually holds about a self-funded group, the number of members, the mean and standard deviation of net claims per member and the attachment factor, and returns the carrier's expected aggregate stop-loss payment and its share of expected claims. It chains HE-FM-ASL-003 (pooled mean and standard deviation), HE-FM-ASL-002 (attachment point), HE-FM-ASL-004 (standardised distance) and HE-FM-ASL-005 (normal expected excess), so its inputs differ from the symbols of the expected payment formula, which needs the attachment point and the distribution of total claims. The calculator version takes Q_z as an input because it has no normal distribution function; the Excel, R and Python versions compute it.

Hazard ratio, interval and cycle probabilities from event counts and person-time

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000795
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Hazard ratio, interval and cycle probabilities from event counts and person-time
has calculator text h_T = d_T / PT_T; h_C = d_C / PT_C; HR = h_T / h_C; SE_beta = sqrt(1 / d_T + 1 / d_C); HR_L = exp(log(HR) - 1.96 * SE_beta); HR_U = exp(log(HR) + 1.96 * SE_beta); p_C = 1 - exp(-h_C * u); p_T = 1 - (1 - p_C)^HR
has common error note Person-time in months with the cycle length in years. If person-time is recorded as 12,000 person-months per arm, the comparator rate is about 0.00833 per person-month, and applying it with u = 1 as if in years gives an annual probability of about 0.0083 instead of 0.0952 (computed here for illustration). The hazard ratio is unaffected, so the error shows only in the absolute probabilities
has description in the module Takes the counts a trial report or registry extract usually gives, the events and person-time at risk in each arm, with the model's cycle length, and returns everything a cohort model needs from them: the two constant hazards, the hazard ratio (HE-FM-HR-005), its approximate 95% interval (HE-FM-HR-006), and the comparator and treatment probabilities of the event in one cycle (HE-FM-HR-004). The steps are chained, so later outputs use earlier ones. The inputs therefore differ from the formulas' variables: the transition probability formula needs p_C and HR, and the function derives both from the raw counts.

Horizontal inequity index from group shares, actual use and need-expected use

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000776

has calculator text R_t = sum_(k=1)^(t-1) [f_k] + f_t / 2; mu = sum_(t=1)^T [f_t * mu_t]; muX = sum_(t=1)^T [f_t * muX_t]; C_M = 2 / mu * sum_(t=1)^T [f_t * mu_t * R_t] - 1; C_N = 2 / muX * sum_(t=1)^T [f_t * muX_t * R_t] - 1; HI = C_M - C_N
has common error note Groups ordered from richest to poorest. has description in the module Takes the inputs a horizontal equity table usually holds, the population share, mean actual use and mean need-expected use of each income group from poorest to richest, and returns the concentration indices of actual and need-expected use and the horizontal inequity index. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: HE-FM-HEQ-003 needs the two indices already computed, and the function builds them from group means, for groups of any size.

Hourly arrival overflow probability and expected cover cost

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000762
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Hourly arrival overflow probability and expected cover cost
has calculator text P_over = 1 - sum_(n=0)^c [lambda_h^n * exp(-lambda_h) / factorial(n)]; E_over = H * P_over; C_over = k * E_over
has common error note Running the check at the daily average rate for a peaked service. Using 2 patients an hour throughout gives £189.55 a day, while the same 48 patients arriving at 3 an hour by day and 1 at night give about £339.11. A model built on the daily average understates the cost of the current service and can misjudge an intervention that mainly removes daytime arrivals
has description in the module Takes the inputs of a capacity check, an hourly arrival rate, the number of arrivals the service can absorb in an hour, the number of hours at that rate and the cost of each overflow hour, and returns the probability that an hour overflows, the expected overflow hours and their expected cost. It sums the Poisson probabilities of HE-FM-ARRP-001 from 0 up to the capacity, takes the complement and scales it by hours and cost, so its inputs differ from the formula's variables. Running it once per rate period and adding the results gives the cost for a day with a peak, as in the article's assessment unit example.

Incremental QALYs and ICER after LOCF from arm utility means and dropout fractions

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000836
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Incremental QALYs and ICER after LOCF from arm utility means and dropout fractions
has calculator text u_T_12_LOCF = tau_T * u_T_6 + (1 - tau_T) * u_T_12; u_C_12_LOCF = tau_C * u_C_6 + (1 - tau_C) * u_C_12; dQ_full = 0.25 * (u_T_0 + 2 * u_T_6 + u_T_12) - 0.25 * (u_C_0 + 2 * u_C_6 + u_C_12); dQ_LOCF = 0.25 * (u_T_0 + 2 * u_T_6 + u_T_12_LOCF) - 0.25 * (u_C_0 + 2 * u_C_6 + u_C_12_LOCF); ICER_full = dC / dQ_full; ICER_LOCF = dC / dQ_LOCF
has common error note Carrying a utility forward past death. Values after death are not missing data. Carrying the last utility forward for a participant who died credits QALYs that were never lived; the function applies only to living participants who stopped providing data
Reading the LOCF ICER as precise. The imputed values carry no uncertainty, so a standard error, confidence interval or acceptability curve computed from LOCF-completed QALYs can be too narrow. The National Research Council panel notes that LOCF may understate uncertainty when imputed values feed an area under the curve, and Faria and colleagues advise against last-value carried forward for missing outcomes in cost-effectiveness analysis
has description in the module Takes the inputs a trial-based cost-effectiveness analysis holds, each arm's mean utility at baseline, 6 months and 12 months with complete follow-up, each arm's fraction of dropouts after the 6-month visit and the incremental cost, and returns the incremental QALYs and ICER with complete data and after LOCF. It applies HE-FM-LOCF-001 to each arm's 12-month mean, then the trapezoidal QALY formula HE-FM-QALY-002 with visits at 0, 0.5 and 1 year, where QALYs are 0.25 times the sum of the baseline mean, twice the 6-month mean and the 12-month mean, and then the ICER of HE-FM-ICER-001. Comparing the two ICERs shows how far the imputation alone moves the result.

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000775
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Kakwani index from group shares of income and health payments
has calculator text LX_t = sum_(k=1)^t [sX_k]; LP_t = sum_(k=1)^t [sP_k]; G_X = 1 - sum_(t=1)^T [f_t * (2 * LX_t - sX_t)]; C_P = 1 - sum_(t=1)^T [f_t * (2 * LP_t - sP_t)]; pi_K = C_P - G_X
has common error note Groups ordered from richest to poorest. Entering the richest group first reverses the curves and flips the sign of every output: the tax data give a Gini coefficient of minus 0.352, a concentration index of minus 0.452 and a Kakwani index of minus 0.100, so a progressive tax appears regressive
has description in the module Takes the inputs a financing incidence table usually holds, the share of the population, of ability to pay and of one health payment in each group, and returns the Gini coefficient of ability to pay, the concentration index of the payment and the Kakwani index. It cumulates the shares, adds the trapezoids of HE-FM-VEQ-002 for each curve, and subtracts as in HE-FM-VEQ-001. Each trapezoid uses the identity L_t plus L_(t-1) equals twice L_t minus s_t, where s_t is the group's own share. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: HE-FM-VEQ-001 needs the two indices already computed, and the function builds them from group shares, for groups of any size.

Likelihood ratio test p-value and critical value from two fitted models

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000786
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Likelihood ratio test p-value and critical value from two fitted models
has calculator text LR = 2 * (ell_1 - ell_0); df = q_1 - q_0; p_value = Q(df / 2, LR / 2); x_crit = Q^-1(alpha | df)
has common error note Running the test on survival curves that are not nested. A Weibull and a log-logistic curve cannot be nested, so the function returns a number with no valid reference distribution. TSD 14 reports that the test has been used this way in past NICE technology appraisals and directs the comparison to AIC and BIC
has description in the module Takes the two fitted models' maximised log-likelihoods and parameter counts and a significance level, and returns the likelihood ratio statistic, its degrees of freedom, the p-value and the critical value. It applies HE-FM-CHISQ-006 and then evaluates the right-hand tail of chi-square, which for most degrees of freedom has no closed form: it is the regularised upper incomplete gamma function with shape df/2 at LR/2, computed numerically by Excel CHISQ.DIST.RT, R pchisq and Python scipy.stats.chi2.sf, while the critical value needs the numerical inverse. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the level alpha is extra, and the outputs include a p-value and a critical value that the formula cannot give.

Partitioned survival occupancy, crossing check, life years and QALYs from PFS and OS curves on a time grid

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000827
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Partitioned survival occupancy, crossing check, life years and QALYs from PFS and OS curves on a time grid
has calculator text P_PD_k = S_OS_k - S_PFS_k; P_D_k = 1 - S_OS_k; n_cross = sum_k [S_PFS_k > S_OS_k]; w_k = (t_k - t_(k-1)) / (1 + d)^((t_(k-1) + t_k) / 2); LY = sum_k [w_k * (S_OS_(k-1) + S_OS_k) / 2]; QALY = sum_k [w_k * (u_PF * (S_PFS_(k-1) + S_PFS_k) / 2 + u_PD * (P_PD_(k-1) + P_PD_k) / 2)]
has common error note Reading the curves only at whole years. An annual grid on steep curves overstates the areas: about 1.1058 QALYs over three years in the standard-care example against about 1.0748 exactly, an overstatement of about 2.9%. A monthly grid brings the lifetime result to within about 0.0003 of the exact 1.35
Setting OS equal to PFS instead of fixing the curves. Setting one curve equal to the other wherever they cross removes the negative shares but hides the inconsistency. In TA285 the ERG considered setting OS equal to PFS in crossed probabilistic draws inappropriate. The function returns no QALYs so that the curves are refitted or the choice is reported
has description in the module Takes PFS and OS as a spreadsheet or a fitted model usually holds them, as survival values on a grid of times from model entry, and returns the state shares at each time, a count of grid times at which PFS lies above OS, and discounted life years and QALYs per person. It applies HE-FM-PSM-001 at each grid time, takes the areas between grid times by the trapezoid rule, discounts each interval at its midpoint and values the areas as in HE-FM-PSM-003. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the formula works at one time t, while the function works on whole curves, a grid and a discount rate, and it declines to value crossed curves rather than patching them.

Per-cycle exit probabilities by time since entry from a survival curve checked against one constant exit probability

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000854
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Per-cycle exit probabilities by time since entry from a survival curve checked against one constant exit probability
has calculator text h_1 = 1 - S_1; h_2 = 1 - S_2 / S_1; h_3 = 1 - S_3 / S_2; h_4 = 1 - S_4 / S_3; h_5 = 1 - S_5 / S_4; q_c = 1 - S_5^(1 / 5); dev = max(abs(h_1 - q_c), abs(h_2 - q_c), abs(h_3 - q_c), abs(h_4 - q_c), abs(h_5 - q_c)); D_c = 1 / q_c
has common error note Constant fitted at one horizon read as a fit at every horizon. The constant 0.093869 matches survival at five years by construction but puts one-year survival at about 0.906 against 0.75, so the first-cycle gap of about 0.156 and the article's third fewer post-event years are hidden when only the five-year value is compared
has description in the module Takes survival at the end of each of the first K cycles since entry to a state, from follow-up of people who entered it or from a model with tunnel states, and returns the exit probability in each cycle among those still in the state, the constant exit probability that a memoryless state fitted to survival at K cycles would use, the largest gap between the two and the mean stay of the fitted memoryless state. Each per-cycle value is h_d of HE-FM-MKA-002 taken from the data, the ratio of adjacent survival values as in HE-FM-CUMH-002, and the constant comes from the rescaling formula HE-FM-TP-003 with the K-cycle survival as the observed value. A memoryless state needs every per-cycle value equal to the constant; a large gap shows that time since entry carries information that one state discards, and the per-cycle values then become the duration-specific inputs of HE-CF-TUN-001. The check is descriptive. With patient-level data Williams and colleagues used a state-arrival extended Cox model with time in the previous state as a covariate to decide whether to accept the assumption. The inputs differ from the formula's symbols, which describe a stay already known to be geometric.
has formalisation level catalogue record

Probabilistic economically justifiable price at a stated probability of cost-effectiveness

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000835
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Probabilistic economically justifiable price at a stated probability of cost-effectiveness
has calculator text EJP_i = (lambda * Delta_E_i - Delta_C_other_i) / U_i; EJP_mean = (lambda * sum_(i=1)^N [Delta_E_i] - sum_(i=1)^N [Delta_C_other_i]) / sum_(i=1)^N [U_i]; P_CE(P) = (1/N) * sum_(i=1)^N [1(EJP_i >= P)]; EJP_alpha = max {P : P_CE(P) >= alpha}
has common error note Assuming the deterministic EJP is cost-effective with high probability. Zeevat and colleagues found that at their base-case EJP of EUR 16.38 for a hypothetical RSV vaccine at EUR 20,000 per QALY, the probability of cost-effectiveness in the Netherlands was 0.44, and at GBP 72.29 at GBP 20,000 per QALY in the United Kingdom it was 0.59. A price chosen to reach a higher probability lies below the EJP
Averaging draw-level EJPs when units vary. Two illustrative draws with 0.80 QALYs and GBP 1,500 of other costs but 20 and 30 packs have draw-level EJPs of GBP 925 and about GBP 616.67, whose mean is about GBP 770.83. Expected net benefit is zero at GBP 740, the GBP 18,500 per patient divided by the mean of 25 packs (computed here for illustration)
has description in the module Takes matched draws from a probabilistic sensitivity analysis, a threshold and a required probability of cost-effectiveness, and returns two prices per unit. EJP_mean is the price at which expected incremental net benefit is zero, the probabilistic EJP of the article. EJP_alpha is the highest price at which at least a share alpha of the draws is cost-effective at lambda, the stricter reading the article describes. Because each draw's net benefit is linear in price, draw i is cost-effective at a price P exactly when P is at or below its own EJP_i, so the probability of cost-effectiveness at P is the share of draw-level EJPs at or above P and the search over prices needs no further model runs. The inputs differ from the formula's variables: the function takes vectors of draws and a probability level and returns an order statistic as well as a closed-form value.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-EJP-001, r implementation of HE-CF-EJP-001, python implementation of HE-CF-EJP-001
has mathematical variable alpha in HE-CF-EJP-001, k in HE-CF-EJP-001, EJP_mean in HE-CF-EJP-001, EJP_alpha in HE-CF-EJP-001, lambda in HE-CF-EJP-001, N in HE-CF-EJP-001
has module record identifier HE-CF-EJP-001
has source in the module National Institute for Health and Care Excellence. NICE technology appraisal and highly specialised technologies guidance: the manual (PMG36). Proportion of days covered from a same-drug fill history with overlap shifting

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000792
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Proportion of days covered from a same-drug fill history with overlap shifting
has calculator text a_1 = f_1; a_i = max(f_i, a_prev + S_prev); C = sum_(i=1)^n [(E - a_i + 1) * (E - a_i + 1 > 0) * (E - a_i + 1 < S_i) + S_i * (E - a_i + 1 >= S_i)]; T = E - f_1 + 1; PDC = C / T
has common error note Shifting the fills in record order instead of date order. If the day-170 fill is processed before the day-121 fill, fill 5 starts on day 170 and fill 4 is pushed to day 200, so only 101 days fall in the period and the PDC drops to about 0.561 instead of 0.833 (computed here for illustration). The fills are sorted by dispensing day before shifting
has description in the module Takes a patient's fill history for one drug, as dispensing days and days' supply, and the last day of the observation period, and returns the PDC. It sorts the fills by date, shifts each early refill to the day after the previous supply ends (HE-FM-PDC-002), counts the covered days on or before the end day (HE-FM-PDC-003) and divides by the days from the first fill to the end day inclusive (HE-FM-PDC-001). The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the formula needs the covered days and the period length, and the function builds both from the raw fills. An optional switch merges overlaps without shifting, for a sensitivity analysis of the overlap rule.

Provider cost indices, direct standardisation check and reversed pairs from group tables

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000879
Type computational function

has calculator text A_A = n_A1 * u_A1 + n_A2 * u_A2; A_B = n_B1 * u_B1 + n_B2 * u_B2; E_A = n_A1 * cbar_1 + n_A2 * cbar_2; E_B = n_B1 * cbar_1 + n_B2 * cbar_2; crude_A = A_A / (n_A1 + n_A2); crude_B = A_B / (n_B1 + n_B2); I_A = 100 * A_A / E_A; I_B = 100 * A_B / E_B; s_1 = (n_A1 + n_B1) / (n_A1 + n_A2 + n_B1 + n_B2); D_A = s_1 * u_A1 + (1 - s_1) * u_A2; D_B = s_1 * u_B1 + (1 - s_1) * u_B2; rev_I = (crude_A - crude_B) * (I_A - I_B) < 0; rev_D = (crude_A - crude_B) * (D_A - D_B) < 0

has description in the module Takes the national mean unit cost of each case group and, for each provider, its cases and its unit cost in each group, and returns each provider's actual cost, expected cost, crude cost per case, cost index and directly standardised cost per case on the pooled mix of all providers, with the number of provider pairs whose crude ranking the index reverses and the number the direct standardisation reverses. The calculator line writes out two providers and two groups; the Excel, R and Python code take any number of each, so the inputs differ from the formula's symbols.

Reported mean and standard error to a fitted beta with its interval and a PSA draw

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000779
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Reported mean and standard error to a fitted beta with its interval and a PSA draw
has calculator text alpha = mu * (mu * (1 - mu) / s^2 - 1), only if s^2 < mu * (1 - mu); beta = (1 - mu) * (mu * (1 - mu) / s^2 - 1); q_lo = F^-1(0.025 | alpha, beta); q_hi = F^-1(0.975 | alpha, beta); p_u = F^-1(u | alpha, beta)
has common error note Normal distribution used for a probability near a boundary. For a probability with mean 0.10 and standard error 0.08, the mean plus or minus 1.96 standard errors runs from about minus 0.057 to 0.257, and about 11% of normal draws fall below zero, which breaks the model logic. The fitted beta keeps every draw above zero
has description in the module Takes the inputs a model usually holds for a bounded parameter, a reported mean, its standard error and a uniform random number, and returns the fitted beta parameters, the central 95% interval and a sampled value. It checks the existence condition, applies the method-of-moments formula HE-FM-BETA-003 and then inverts the cumulative beta distribution numerically, which has no closed form: Excel BETA.INV searches for the value whose cumulative probability equals the target, and R qbeta and Python scipy.stats.beta.ppf return the same inverse. The inputs therefore differ from the formula's variables: the uniform draw u and the interval levels are extra, and the outputs include quantiles that the formula cannot give.

ROC threshold for a minimal important change from patients' change scores and anchor classification

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000823
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of ROC threshold for a minimal important change from patients' change scores and anchor classification
has calculator text Se_c = sum_i [I(change_i >= c) * improved_i] / sum_i [improved_i]; Sp_c = sum_i [I(change_i < c) * (1 - improved_i)] / sum_i [1 - improved_i]; M_c = (1 - Se_c) + (1 - Sp_c); cutoff = argmin_c M_c
has common error note Counting only the about-the-same group as the reference for the ROC. The ROC analysis in the article compares the 90 improved patients with all 110 others; restricting the reference to the 70 rating themselves about the same, as the 95 per cent limit does, changes specificity at every cut-off and can move the threshold
has description in the module Computes sensitivity, specificity, the misclassification sum and the Youden index at every candidate cut-off from patients' change scores and their anchor classification, and returns the ROC threshold, the candidate with the smallest misclassification sum (the lowest such candidate when two tie). Seeded one-step agent simulation checked against the analytic infection probability

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000861
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Seeded one-step agent simulation checked against the analytic infection probability
has calculator text P_3 = 1 - (1 - p)^k_3; P_2 = 1 - (1 - p)^k_2; Phat_3 = n_3 / N_agents; Phat_2 = n_2 / N_agents; MCSE_3 = sqrt(P_3 * (1 - P_3) / N_agents); ok = abs(Phat_3 - P_3) <= 3 * MCSE_3; D_hat = Phat_3 - Phat_2
has common error note Fresh seeds for the programme. Drawing new numbers for the programme leaves the expected share averted at 0.081 but raises the variance of the per-agent difference about 4.7 fold (HE-ER-ABM-003), so for 10,000 agents the standard error of the share averted rises from about 0.0027 to about 0.0059
has description in the module Runs a small seeded agent simulation of one step and checks it against the analytic infection probability HE-FM-ABM-001. It takes a number of susceptible agents, a per-contact transmission probability, the number of infectious contacts per agent under a comparator and under a programme, and a base seed, and returns the simulated and analytic shares infected under each, the Monte Carlo standard error of the simulated share, a pass flag and the simulated infections averted per agent. Each agent i draws one uniform number per contact from its own stream, seeded with seed + i, and is infected when any draw is below p, so the simulation works contact by contact and never uses the formula itself. The programme removes each agent's last contact and keeps the draws for the others, so the two strategies share common random numbers.

Sequential incremental benefit-cost comparison of mutually exclusive options

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000780
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Sequential incremental benefit-cost comparison of mutually exclusive options
has calculator text sort options by PVC_j ascending; d = cheapest option; for each later option j: BCR_jd = (PVB_j - PVB_d) / (PVC_j - PVC_d); d = j if PVC_j > PVC_d and BCR_jd > 1; NPV_d = PVB_d - PVC_d
has common error note Ranking the options by their own ratios. Picking the option with the highest ratio against doing nothing selects X in the base case, with a ratio of 2.0 against 1.56, and loses £1.0 million of net present value. Comparing each option with the next cheaper option rather than with the current choice can also select an option that loses to an earlier one
has description in the module Takes the present-value benefits and costs of any number of mutually exclusive options, including doing nothing, and returns the option with the largest net present value by applying the incremental ratio HE-FM-BCRX-002 step by step. The options are sorted by cost; the cheapest becomes the current choice, and each more costly option replaces it only if its incremental ratio over the current choice exceeds one. The inputs are lists of options rather than the two named options X and Y of the formula, and each comparison is made against the current choice, not against the next cheaper option.

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000833
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Standard, Wagstaff and Erreygers concentration indices from group values, shares and scale bounds
has calculator text r_j = sum_(k=1)^(j-1) [f_k] + f_j / 2; mu = sum_(j=1)^J [f_j * h_j]; cov_hr = sum_(j=1)^J [f_j * (h_j - mu) * (r_j - 0.5)]; C = 2 * cov_hr / mu; W = 2 * (b - a) * cov_hr / ((b - mu) * (mu - a)); E = 8 * cov_hr / (b - a); C_s = -2 * cov_hr / (a + b - mu); W_s = -W; E_s = -E
has common error note Groups entered from richest to poorest. Reversing the order reverses every rank and so every sign: the illness example returns C of 0.25 and E of 0.24, which reads as illness concentrated among the better-off
has description in the module Takes the mean of a bounded health variable in each socioeconomic group, ordered from poorest to richest, the share of the population in each group and the bounds of the measurement scale, and returns the standard concentration index with its generalised Wagstaff and Erreygers corrections, plus the same three indices for the mirror variable. It builds each group's fractional rank as the cumulative share before the group plus half its own share, then the population-weighted mean and covariance, and then applies HE-FM-HINQ-005, HE-FM-CIX-001 in the generalised form of Erreygers and Van Ourti, and HE-FM-CIX-002. The inputs therefore differ from the formulas' variables, which take C and mu as given. Individual records can be entered one per row with their survey weights as the shares.

It chains HE-FM-AP-001, which sets the instalment at the financing rate, and HE-FM-AP-002, which values the instalments at the discount rate, so the second step uses the result of the first and the two rates play different parts. A financing rate of zero, as in an interest-free split, is handled as a separate case because the general instalment formula then divides zero by zero.

Trial summary data to an illustrative QALY gain through Cohen's d

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000790
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Trial summary data to an illustrative QALY gain through Cohen's d
has calculator text s_p = sqrt(((n_1 - 1) * s_1^2 + (n_2 - 1) * s_2^2) / (n_1 + n_2 - 2)); d = (xbar_1 - xbar_2) / s_p; lnOR = 1.814 * d; OR = exp(lnOR); p_1 = p_0 * OR / (1 - p_0 + p_0 * OR); Delta_Q = (p_1 - p_0) * (u_R - u_N) * L
has common error note Carrying the standardised effect into the model as a probability or utility. Reading a d of 0.54 as 54 percentage points more responders, or adding it to a utility value, overstates the effect many times over. The chain gives about 0.23 more responders and a QALY gain of about 0.019, and only through a comparator response rate and utility values that need their own sensitivity analysis
has description in the module Takes the summary data a trial report gives, group means, standard deviations and sizes, together with the external inputs a cost-utility model needs, a comparator response probability, utility values for responders and non-responders and a duration, and returns an illustrative QALY gain per person. It chains four steps: Cohen's d from HE-FM-CD-001, the logistic conversion to an odds ratio from HE-FM-CD-002, the intervention response probability from the comparator probability and the odds ratio as in HE-FM-NMA-004, and the extra proportion responding multiplied by the utility difference and the duration. The inputs therefore differ from either formula's variables.

Two risk strata run as separate cohorts against one averaged cohort, with life expectancy, QALY gains and ICERs

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000866
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Two risk strata run as separate cohorts against one averaged cohort, with life expectancy, QALY gains and ICERs
has calculator text q_bar = w * q_low + (1 - w) * q_high; L_low_0 = 1 / q_low - 0.5; L_low_1 = 1 / ((1 - e) * q_low) - 0.5; L_high_0 = 1 / q_high - 0.5; L_high_1 = 1 / ((1 - e) * q_high) - 0.5; L_avg_0 = 1 / q_bar - 0.5; L_avg_1 = 1 / ((1 - e) * q_bar) - 0.5; dQ_strat = u * (w * (L_low_1 - L_low_0) + (1 - w) * (L_high_1 - L_high_0)); dQ_avg = u * (L_avg_1 - L_avg_0); ICER_strat = c / dQ_strat; ICER_avg = c / dQ_avg; LE_strat_0 = w * L_low_0 + (1 - w) * L_high_0; under_LE = 1 - L_avg_0 / LE_strat_0; under_dQ = 1 - dQ_avg / dQ_strat; over_ICER = ICER_avg / ICER_strat - 1
has common error note Running the average patient. Feeding the mean probability of 0.15 into one cohort gives an ICER of GBP 15,000 per QALY against GBP 8,333 from the stratified runs, 80% too high, and hides the fivefold difference between the stratum ICERs of GBP 5,000 and GBP 25,000 that the article reports
has description in the module Takes the share of a population in a low-risk stratum, the annual death probabilities of a low-risk and a high-risk stratum, a proportional cut in those probabilities from treatment, a utility per year alive and a one-off treatment cost, and returns the life expectancies of six cohort runs (each stratum and one averaged cohort, with and without treatment), the population QALY gain and ICER from the stratified runs, the same results from the averaged cohort, and the relative errors of the averaged cohort. It carries out the article's worked example. Each stratum gain is the difference of two values of the live L(q), HE-FM-ABS-001 less half a cycle, and equals e divided by (1 minus e) q because the half cycle cancels (derived here); the stratified QALY gain weights those gains with the shares of HE-FM-ATE-001, the ICERs follow HE-FM-ICER-001, and the life-expectancy shortfall is gap_L of HE-FM-COHM-001. The calculator line uses the closed form 1/q minus one half; the Excel, R and Python code instead run the Alive and Dead cohort trace numerically for a fixed number of cycles, so they also check the closed form, and the code takes any number of strata. The inputs differ from the formula's symbols, which describe one comparison at a time.
has formalisation level catalogue record
has implementation excel implementation of HE-CF-COHM-001, r implementation of HE-CF-COHM-001, python implementation of HE-CF-COHM-001

Two-sector Baumol scenario from a starting share of hours

https://healtheconomics.wiki/ontologies/hemo/HEMO_5000773
Type computational function
assumes Assumption of Two-sector Baumol scenario from a starting share of hours
has calculator text C_h = ((1+g_w)/(1+g_h))^T; C_o = ((1+g_w)/(1+g_o))^T; R_T = C_h / C_o; x_0 = s_h0 / (1 - s_h0); x_T = x_0 * R_T; s_hT = x_T / (1 + x_T); q_o = (1 - s_hT) / (1 - s_h0) * (1+g_o)^T; q_h = s_hT / s_h0 * (1+g_h)^T
has common error note Reading higher unit costs as lower real output. A rising health care share of spending can be read as health care crowding out other output. In the twenty-year example the share rises from 10 to 13 per cent, yet real output in both sectors is about 43.6 per cent higher, the sense in which Baumol argued that rising health costs remain affordable when productivity grows elsewhere
has description in the module Takes the inputs of a simple two-sector scenario, the starting share of hours in health care, annual productivity growth in each sector, annual wage growth and a horizon, and returns the unit cost indices, the relative cost, the hours ratio and share needed to hold the output mix, and real output growth in each sector. It chains HE-FM-BAUM-002 and HE-FM-BAUM-003, so the later steps use the results of the earlier ones, and its inputs differ from the formulas' symbols: it starts from a share of hours rather than a ratio and includes the wage, which drops out of the relative cost. Holding total hours fixed, it checks that both sectors still produce more.
